Quantum cluster algebra, braid moves and quantum virtual Grothendieck ring Thanks: ⋆ K.-H. Lee was partially supported by a grant from the Simons Foundation (#712100). Thanks: † S.-j. Oh was supported by the Ministry of Education of the Republic of Korea and the National Research Foundation of Korea (NRF-2022R1A2C1004045).
Kyu-Hwan Lee ⋆, Se-jin Oh †

Abstract
. In this paper, we study the quantum virtual Grothendieck ring, denoted by 𝔎 q ​ ( 𝔤 ) \mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}) , which was introduced in [ 39 ] , and further investigated in [ 26 , 25 ] . Our approach involves examining this ring from two perspectives: first, by considering its connection to quantum cluster algebras of non-skew-symmetric types; and second, by exploring its relevance to categorification theory. We specifically focus on (i) the homomorphisms that arise from braid moves, particularly 4-moves and 6-moves, in the braid group; and (ii) the quantum Laurent positivity phenomena, which has not yet been proven for non-skew-symmetric types. As applications of our results, we derive the substitution formulas for non-skew-symmetric types discussed in [ 11 ] for skew-symmetric types, and demonstrate that any truncated element in a heart subring, denoted by 𝔎 q , Q ​ ( 𝔤 ) \mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g}) , which corresponds to a simple module over the quiver Hecke algebra R 𝔤 R^{\mathfrak{g}} , possesses coefficients in ℤ ⩾ 0 [ q ± 1 / 2 ] \mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}[q^{\pm 1/2}] . This result is particularly interesting because it implies that each truncated Kirillov–Reshetikhin polynomial in 𝔎 q , Q ​ ( 𝔤 ) \mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g}) and each element in the standard basis 𝖤 q ​ ( 𝔤 ) \mathsf{E}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}) of the entire ring 𝔎 q ​ ( 𝔤 ) \mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}) have coefficients also in ℤ ⩾ 0 [ q ± 1 / 2 ] \mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}[q^{\pm 1/2}] . Since (truncated) Kirillov–Reshetikhin polynomials can be obtained using a quantum cluster algebra algorithm and appear as quantum cluster variables, they provide compelling evidence in support of the quantum Laurent positivity conjecture in non-skew-symmetric types.



1.
Introduction

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a complex finite-dimensional simple Lie algebra, which has an index set
I
and a Cartan matrix
\mathsf{C}
. The quantum loop algebra
U_{q}(\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{g})
associated with

\mathfrak{g}
has been extensively studied since its emergence. This is mainly due to the fact that its representation theory exhibits a rich structure characterized by monoidality
and rigidity. Although the category
\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
of type
\mathbf{1}
-representations over
U_{q}(\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{g})
is not braided, it is well-known that the Grothendieck ring
K(\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}})

is commutative.

By the motivation originated from
\mathcal{W}
-algebras, Frenkel–Reshetikhin
[
9
]
constructed the
q
-character
monomorphism

\chi_{q}:K(\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[Y_{i,w}^{\pm 1}]_{i\in I,w\in\mathbb{C}(q)^{\times}}
which also explains the commutativity of

K(\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}})\simeq\chi_{q}(K(\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}))=:\mathcal{K}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathfrak{g}})
.
Nakajima
[
48
]
and Varagnolo–Vasserot
[
55
]
then employed the geometry of quiver varieties to construct the non-commutative
t
-quantization, denoted as
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}})
, of
\mathcal{K}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}})
for a simply-laced type
\mathbf{g}
. This construction is known as the
quantum Grothendieck ring
and highlights the non-braided property of
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}})
recovers
\mathcal{K}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}})
when
t=1
.

The quantum Grothendieck ring admits a remarkable Kazhdan–Lusztig algorithm for
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}}
, offering insights into the Jordan–Hölder multiplicity
P_{m,m^{\prime}}
of a simple module
L(m^{\prime})
in a standard module
E(m)
in
K(\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}})
. Nakajima established this connection through the formulation of the Kazhdan–Lusztig type polynomial
P_{m.m^{\prime}}(t)
in
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}})
, with its specialization at
t=1
yielding the desired
P_{m,m^{\prime}}
.

To be more specific, he constructed bases
\mathbf{E}_{t}(\mathbf{g})=\{E_{t}(m)\}
and
\mathbf{L}_{t}(\mathbf{g})=\{L_{t}(m)\}
for
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}})
, consisting of
(q,t)
-characters
of standard modules and simple modules, respectively. He proved the uni-triangular property that exists between these two bases:

(1.1)

\displaystyle E_{t}(m)=L_{t}(m)+\sum_{m^{\prime};m^{\prime}<_{N}m}P_{m,m^{\prime}}(t)L_{t}(m^{\prime})\quad\text{ for some }P_{m,m^{\prime}}(t)\in t\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[t]

in
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}})
. We call
\mathbf{E}_{t}(\mathbf{g})
the
standard basis
and
\mathbf{L}_{t}(\mathbf{g})
the
canonical basis
.

Taking a significant stride forward, Nakajima went on to establish that every coefficient polynomial of
L_{t}(m)
,
E_{t}(m)
, and the polynomial
P_{m,m^{\prime}}(t)
resides in
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}[t^{\pm 1/2}]
. Moreover, he proved that the specializations of
L_{t}(m)
and
E_{t}(m)
at
t=1
yield the
q
-character
\chi_{q}(L(m))
for the simple module
L(m)
and the
q
-character
\chi_{q}(E(m))
for the standard module
E(m)
in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathbf{g}}
, respectively. These findings collectively underscore the profound nature of the algorithm he had unveiled.

In cases where
\mathfrak{g}
is non-simply-laced, a fully-developed geometry is lacking, rendering the direct application of the framework for
\mathbf{g}
impractical.
To address this, Hernandez

[
18
]
introduced a conjectural Kazhdan–Lusztig algorithm for all
\mathfrak{g}
, using the
quantum Cartan matrix

\mathsf{C}(q)
associated with
\mathfrak{g}
. He devised
a non-commutative
t
-quantization, also denoted as
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathfrak{g})
, of
\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{g})
and constructed a basis
\mathbf{F}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
for
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathfrak{g})
through a special
algorithm known as the
t
-algorithm
. It is noteworthy that the
t
-algorithm can be viewed as a
t
-quantization of the Frenkel–Mukhin algorithm
in
[
8
]
, utilizing
\mathsf{C}(q)
.

Subsequently, Hernandez constructed bases
\mathbf{E}_{t}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\mathbf{L}_{t}(\mathfrak{g})
for
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathfrak{g})
, adhering to (
1.1
), and expected them to also exhibit
the same positivity as
L_{t}(m)
,
E_{t}(m)
, and
P_{m,m^{\prime}}(t)
. Particularly, in
[
19
]
, he showed that the
(q,t)
-character
L_{t}(Y_{i,w})
of a

fundamental representation

L(Y_{i,w})
manifests the desired
quantum positivity
. This implies that every coefficient polynomial of
L(Y_{i,w})

resides in
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}[t^{\pm 1/2}]
, consequently establishing the quantum positivity of elements in
\mathbf{E}_{t}(\mathfrak{g})
as well.

The quantum cluster algebra
\mathscr{A}_{q}
, introduced by Berenstein–Fomin–Zelevinsky in
[
6
,
2
]
, serves as a crucial framework for the investigation
of the dual-canonical/upper-global basis
\mathbf{B}^{\rm up}
introduced by Lusztig/Kashiwara, through a combinatorial point of view. The algebra
\mathscr{A}_{q}
is a
non-commutative
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
-subalgebra in the skew field of
\mathbb{Q}(q^{1/2})(Z_{i})_{i\in\mathsf{K}}
, generated by cluster variables. These variables are derived
from the initial cluster
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{K}}
through a sequence of quantum exchange relations known as
mutations
. Despite the non-trivial nature
of these mutations, the
quantum Laurent phenomenon
, proven
in
[
6
,
2
]
, assures that
\mathscr{A}_{q}
remains contained in the (non-commutative) Laurent polynomial ring
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}][Z_{i}^{\pm 1}]_{i\in\mathsf{K}}
.

Another interesting feature of
\mathscr{A}_{q}
, which has been confirmed for skew-symmetric cases (see
[
4
]
), is the

quantum Laurent positivity conjecture
. This conjecture asserts that every cluster variable indeed belongs to
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}[q^{\pm 1/2}][Z_{i}^{\pm 1}]_{i\in\mathsf{K}}
.

Building upon the earlier discussion, it is worthwhile to emphasize that the algebras
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\mathscr{A}_{q}
exhibit similar quantum Laurent positivity phenomena.
The connection between these two structures is further underscored by the notable works of Hernandez–Leclerc in
[
21
,
22
,
23
]
.
This relationship gains additional support from the results
in
[
3
,
27
,
38
,
49
,
33
,
36
,
10
]
, which collectively establish that
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathfrak{g})
possesses a quantum cluster algebra structure.

In a recent development, Fujita, Hernandez, Oya, and the second named author of this paper have successfully resolved the quantum positivity conjectures for
L_{t}(m)
and

P_{m,m^{\prime}}(t)
in
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathfrak{g})
for non-simply-laced Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}
. This achievement was made possible through the utilization of the quantum cluster algebra
structure of
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathfrak{g})
and the application of a technique known as the
propagation of positivity

[
10
,
11
]
. Let us explain their results more precisely.

For each specific
\mathfrak{g}
, we define
\mathbf{g}_{{\rm fin}}
of simply-laced type as follows:

(\mathfrak{g},\mathbf{g}_{{\rm fin}})=(B_{n},A_{2n-1}),\ (C_{n},D_{n+1}),\ (F_{4},E_{6}),\ (G_{2},D_{4})\ \text{ and }\ \mathfrak{g}=\mathbf{g}_{{\rm fin}}\ \ \text{otherwise}.

In their work
[
10
]
, it was established that the algebras
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{g}})
and
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathbf{g}_{{\rm fin}}})
are isomorphic, with their
presentations characterized as a
boson-extension
of the negative half of the quantum group
U^{-}_{q}(\mathbf{g}_{{{\rm fin}}})
(refer also to
[
25
, (1.7)]
).
Here,
\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{g}}
denotes the
skeleton category
, also known as the
Hernandez–Leclerc
subcategory. Taking a further step in
[
11
]
, they proved that the isomorphisms between
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{g}})
and

\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathbf{g}_{{\rm fin}}})
can be interpreted as sequences of mutations-permutations between
adapted
sequences of indices. Moreover, they extended these isomorphisms to the skew fields of their quantum tori, introducing the
substitution formula
.

The implications of these isomorphisms are far-reaching, leading to several applications:

(a)

Propagating positivity
: Using the isomorphisms to propagate positivity from type
\mathbf{g}_{{\rm fin}}
to type
\mathfrak{g}
,
they proved the quantum positivity conjectures for
L_{t}(m)
and
P_{m,m^{\prime}}(t)
in non-simply-laced type
\mathfrak{g}
.

(b)

Recovery of
q
-character
: Using the monoidal categorification result presented in
[
36
]
and the isomorphisms, they proved that

L_{t}(m)
recovers the
q
-character of a simple module
L(m)
at
q=1
, provided it is
reachable
.

(c)

Interplay between categories
: The substitution formula revealed intriguing behaviors among elements in
\mathbf{L}_{t}(\mathfrak{g})
and
\mathbf{L}_{t}(\mathbf{g}_{{\rm fin}})
.

In summary, the algebraic structure of
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{g}})
exhibits (skew-)symmetry, irrespective of the inherent asymmetry in
\mathfrak{g}
. Moreover, the observed quantum positivity
phenomena of
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{g}})
are connected to those witnessed in the context of the
skew-symmetric
quantum cluster algebra
\mathscr{A}_{q}
.

Building upon the aforementioned observations, Kashiwara and the second named author of this paper introduced a non-commutative Laurent polynomial ring
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
over

\mathbb{A}\mathbin{:=}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
for every
\mathfrak{g}
. Referred to as the
quantum virtual Grothendieck ring
, the ring
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is particularly noteworthy as

\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathbf{g})\simeq\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathbf{g}})
when
\mathbf{g}
is of simply-laced type. Note that the construction of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
can be seen as a
\mathsf{C}(t)
-analogue
of Hernandez’s construction for
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\mathfrak{g}})
, where
\mathsf{C}(t)
is another quantization of
\mathsf{C}
known as the
t
-quantized Cartan matrix
.

The main results established in
[
39
,
26
]
include:

(i)

Quantum cluster algebra structures
: The algebra
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, along with various subrings, possesses quantum cluster algebra structures. Importantly, the (a)symmetries of these structures coincide with those of the underlying Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.

(ii)

Bases and relations
: Bases
\mathsf{F}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
,
\mathsf{E}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, and
\mathsf{L}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
are identified in
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, and they exhibit similar relations to those in
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathfrak{g})
, including (
1.1
). Notably,
\mathsf{E}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is termed the
standard basis
, and
\mathsf{L}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
the
canonical basis
, mirroring the naming in the
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathfrak{g})
cases.

Conjecture I

(
[
26
]
)
.

(a)

In case where
L_{q}(m)
is real, meaning that
L_{q}(m)^{2}=q^{a/2}L_{q}(m^{2})
for some
a\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
, every coefficient polynomial of
L_{q}(m)
resides in
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
.

(b)

For a
Kirillov–Reshetikhin (KR)
monomial
m\in\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, the element
L_{q}(m)
in
\mathsf{L}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
coincides with
F_{q}(m)
in
\mathsf{F}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
consisting of real elements, where
F_{q}(m)
is a KR-polynomial
(
see
§
5.3
for the definition
)
.

In particular, combining (
a
) and (
b
), we expect that each coefficient polynomial of a KR-polynomial
F_{q}(m)
is
in
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
.

We remark here that each basis element
F_{q}(m)
in
\mathsf{F}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
can be obtained from the
q
-algorithm,
which is a
\mathsf{C}(t)
-analogue of
t
-algorithm and is defined inductively. Thus determining whether the coefficient polynomials of
F_{q}(m)
are in
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
or not
is quite intricate.

In this paper, we study further the quantum virtual Grothendieck ring
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, utilizing its quantum cluster algebra structure. It is important to note
that
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is generated by a countably infinite set of
fundamental
polynomials, denoted as
\{F_{q}(X_{i,p})\}_{(i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}}
and parameterized by a specific
index set
{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
(see (
5.2
)).

For each Dynkin quiver
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
associated with the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
(see §
5.5
), the
heart
subring
\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g})
of

\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is generated by the fundamental polynomials
\{F_{q}(X_{i,p})\}_{(i,p)\in\Gamma^{Q}_{0}\subset{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}}

(see
[
12
,
25
]
). The utilization of the heart subrings yields the following homomorphisms
[
22
,
10
,
26
]
:

(a)

Isomorphism
\Psi_{Q}
:
An
\mathbb{A}
-algebra isomorphism, denoted by
\Psi_{Q}
, is established between the integral form
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathfrak{n})
of the quantum unipotent coordinate ring
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathfrak{n})
and the heart subring
\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g})
. Importantly, this isomorphism maps the
normalized dual-canonical/upper-global basis

\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\rm up}
to the canonical basis
\mathsf{L}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g})\mathbin{:=}\mathsf{L}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\cap\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g})
of
\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g})
.

(b)

Automorphism
\Psi_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}
:
For any pair of Dynkin quivers
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
and
Q^{\prime}=(\triangle,\xi)
, there exists an automorphism denoted by
\Psi_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}
of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
. This automorphism sends the heart subring
\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{g})
to
\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g})
and respects the canonical basis
\mathsf{L}_{q}
.

Note that the integral form
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathfrak{n})
of the quantum unipotent coordinate ring
\mathcal{A}_{q}(\mathfrak{n})
is isomorphic to the quantum cluster algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}}
(
[
13
,
15
]
). The initial seed of
\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}}
can be constructed through the combinatorics of a reduced sequence
i
associated with the longest element
w_{\circ}
of the Weyl group
\mathsf{W}
.

As a main result, Theorem
5.21
establishes, through the use of isomorphisms and the degrees of quantum cluster monomials, that the isomorphism
\Psi_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}:\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{g})\mathop{\xrightarrow[\raisebox{0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.90417pt]{$\scriptstyle{}$}]{{\raisebox{-1.80835pt}[0.0pt][-1.80835pt]{$\mspace{2mu}\sim\mspace{2mu}$}}}}\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
obtained by restricting
\Psi_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}
to the
\xi^{\prime}
-negative subring

\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{g})
of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
(refer to Definition
5.8
) is compatible with sequences of mutations-permutations between the seeds associated with infinite sequences
{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}
and
{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}
in
I^{(\infty)}
respectively adapted to
Q^{\prime}
and
Q
(see (
5.11
)).

The proof of Theorem
5.21
involves the following steps:

(a)

Braid moves as mutations-permutations
: The interpretation of braid moves as mutations-permutations is established.

(b)

Analysis of degrees of quantum cluster monomials
: The behaviors of degrees of quantum cluster monomials concerning mutations-permutations corresponding to braid moves are analyzed. This analysis includes the study of
4
-moves for types
B_{n},C_{n},F_{4}
and
6
-moves for type
G_{2}
, involving more complicated computations compared to the investigations of
2
-moves and
3
-moves presented in
[
11
]
.

Through this detailed analysis, it is proved that the isomorphism
\Psi_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}
induces a corresponding bijection between their quantum cluster monomials, establishing the compatibility highlighted in Theorem
5.21
.

As an application, we extend the automorphisms
\Psi_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}
to substitution formulas
\widetilde{\Psi}_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}
for the variables in the fractional skew field
\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}_{q})
of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
(Theorem
5.30
). These substitution formulas, derived from mutations-permutations and consequently being automorphisms, involve non-trivial fractions among Laurent polynomials. Independent of the quantum Laurent phenomena, these substitution formulas yield Laurent polynomials in
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
and respect the canonical basis
\mathsf{L}_{q}
.

Exploring these substitution formulas reveals highly non-trivial relations among elements in
\mathsf{L}_{q}
. For concrete examples of these formulas, particularly for type
B_{2}
, we refer the reader to Appendix
A
. The investigation of the substitution formulas offers valuable insights into relationships among elements in the canonical basis
\mathsf{L}_{q}
.

Another main result (Theorem
7.1
) of this paper is a proof of quantum positivity for:
(a) fundamental polynomials
F_{q}(X_{i,p})=L_{q}(X_{i,p})
,
(b) elements in the standard basis
\mathsf{E}_{q}
,
(c) truncated KR-polynomials in
(\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g}))_{\leqslant\xi}
.
The quantum cluster algebra algorithm generates fundamental polynomials
L_{q}(X_{i,p})
and truncated KR-polynomials
F_{q}(m)_{\leqslant\xi}
, starting from their highest dominant monomials
\underline{X_{i,p}}
and
\underline{m}
(
[
23
,
3
,
26
]
). This result serves as robust evidence supporting the quantum positivity conjecture for non-skew-symmetric types.

The main argument of the proof involves the categorification theory of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathfrak{n})
through the category
R\text{-}\mathrm{gmod}
of finite-dimensional graded modules over the quiver Hecke algebra
R
(
[
41
,
52
]
).
Recently, Kashiwara, Kim, Park, and the second named author introduced the concept of a
Laurent family
of simple modules over
R
and proved that each Laurent family exhibits the quantum Laurent positivity phenomena. This positivity is observed with respect to the basis of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathfrak{n})
that corresponds to the isomorphism classes of simple modules over
R
through the process of categorification (
[
37
]
).

The proof of Theorem
7.1
employs the following key ingredients:

(i)

The isomorphisms among
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{A}}(R\text{-}\mathrm{gmod})
,
\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g})
, and
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathfrak{n})
(see (
7
)).

(ii)

The truncation isomorphism
(\cdot)_{\leqslant\xi}:\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g})\mathop{\xrightarrow[\raisebox{0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.90417pt]{$\scriptstyle{}$}]{{\raisebox{-1.80835pt}[0.0pt][-1.80835pt]{$\mspace{2mu}\sim\mspace{2mu}$}}}}(\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g}))_{\leqslant\xi}
(see (
5.8
)).

(iii)

The structure of KR-polynomials (see Theorem
5.6
).

(iv)

The fact that KR-polynomials in
\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g})

can be understood as certain characters
of determinantal modules over
R
(
[
32
,
25
]
).

With these ingredients, we show in Theorem
7.1
that:

(a)

The set of KR-polynomials
\{F_{q}(m^{(i)}[p,\xi_{i}])\ |\ (i,p)\in\Gamma^{Q}_{0}\}
is the set of
such characters
of the Laurent family

\mathcal{L}\mathbin{:=}\{M(w_{k}^{\textbf{{i}}}\varpi_{i_{k}},\varpi_{i_{k}})\}
of
determinantal modules
for any reduced sequence
i
of
w_{\circ}

adapted to

Q=(\triangle,\xi)
.

(b)

Any element
\mathsf{X}_{\leqslant\xi}\in(\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}(\mathfrak{g}))_{\leqslant\xi}
corresponding to a simple module
X
over
R
is quantum positive (see Example
7.3
for instances).

As special cases, we obtain the desired result (Corollary
7.2
) that fundamental polynomials, elements in the standard basis
\mathsf{E}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, and truncated KR-polynomials in
(\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q})(\mathfrak{g})_{\leqslant\xi}
are quantum positive.

This paper is structured as follows:
Section
2
provides a review of the fundamental concepts in the theory of quantum cluster algebras, covering essential topics such as the degrees of quantum cluster monomials and the valued quiver associated with an exchange matrix.
Section
3
explores the mutations-permutations corresponding to moves in the braid group of
\mathfrak{g}
. It contains intricate computations and makes crucial observations concerning the behaviors of degrees of quantum cluster monomials with respect to these mutations-permutations.
We allocate considerable space to this section because the computations are quite important for later parts.
Section
4
investigates the connection between mutations-permutations corresponding to moves and the basis
\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{{\rm up}}
in
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathfrak{g})
through the isomorphism
\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}}\mathop{\xrightarrow[\raisebox{0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.90417pt]{$\scriptstyle{}$}]{{\raisebox{-1.80835pt}[0.0pt][-1.80835pt]{$\mspace{2mu}\sim\mspace{2mu}$}}}}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathfrak{n})
.
In Section
5
, we establishes the compatibility between the isomorphism
\Phi_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}
and mutations-permutations corresponding to moves. This compatibility is then applied to the analysis of substitution formulas.
Section
6
reviews the categorification of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathfrak{n})
via
R\text{-}\mathrm{gmod}
and discusses the results from
[
37
]
related to the concept of a Laurent family.
Section
7
presents the proof of the quantum positivity of fundamental polynomials, the standard basis
\mathsf{E}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, and truncated KR-polynomials in
(\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q})(\mathfrak{g})_{\leqslant\xi}
.

Convention

Throughout this paper, we use the following convention.

\bullet

For a statement
\mathtt{P}
, we set
\delta(\mathtt{P})
to be
1
or
0

depending on whether
\mathtt{P}
is true or not. In particular, we
set
\delta_{i,j}\mathbin{:=}\delta(i=j)
(the Kronecker’s delta).

\bullet

For a totally ordered set
J=\{\cdots<j_{-1}<j_{0}<j_{1}<j_{2}\cdots\}
, write

\prod_{j\in J}^{\to}A_{j}\mathbin{:=}\cdots A_{j_{-1}}A_{j_{0}}A_{j_{1}}A_{j_{2}}\cdots.

\bullet

For
a\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
, we set
[a]_{+}\mathbin{:=}\max(a,0)
. Note that

a=[a]_{+}-[-a]_{+}
.

\bullet

We set
\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}\mathbin{:=}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 1}
and
\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}_{0}\mathbin{:=}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}
.

\bullet

For a set
A
, we denote by
\mathfrak{S}_{A}
the group of permutations of
A
. In particular, if
A=\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
, we write
\sigma_{k}
for the simple transposition of
k
and
k+1
in
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathbb{N}}\mspace{1mu}
.

\bullet

We set
q
to be an indeterminate and

\mathbb{A}\mathbin{:=}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
, where
q^{1/2}
denotes the formal square root of

q
. For an
\mathbb{A}
-algebra
\mathcal{R}
and elements
X
and
Y
in
\mathcal{R}
, we write
X\cong Y
if there exists
a\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
such that
X=q^{a/2}Y
.

2.
Quantum cluster algebra structure

2.1.
Quantum cluster algebra

Let
\mathsf{K}
be a (possibly infinite) countable index set with a decomposition

\mathsf{K}=\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{ex}}\sqcup\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{fr}}
. We call
\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{ex}}
the set of exchangeable indices and
\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{ex}}
the set of frozen indices. Let
\Lambda=(\Lambda_{i,j})_{i,j\in\mathsf{K}}

be a skew-symmetric
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
-valued matrix.

Definition 2.1
.

We denote by
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda)
the
\mathbb{A}
-algebra
generated by
\{Z_{k}^{\pm 1}\}_{k\in\mathsf{K}}
subject to the following defining
relations:

\displaystyle Z_{j}Z_{j}^{-1}=Z_{j}^{-1}Z_{j}\quad\text{and}\quad Z_{i}Z_{j}=q^{\Lambda_{i,j}}Z_{j}Z_{i}\quad\text{for }i,j\in\mathsf{K}.

We define the
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
-algebra anti-involution
\overline{(\cdot)}
of
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda)
, called the
bar-involution
,
by

(2.1)

\displaystyle\overline{q^{1/2}}\mathbin{:=}q^{-1/2},\qquad\overline{Z_{k}}\mathbin{:=}Z_{k},

for all
k\in\mathsf{K}
.
For
\mathbf{a}=(\mathbf{a}_{i})_{i\in\mathsf{K}}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\oplus\mathsf{K}}
, we define the element

Z^{\mathbf{a}}
of
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda)
as

Z^{\mathbf{a}}\mathbin{:=}q^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i>j}\mathbf{a}_{i}\mathbf{a}_{j}\Lambda_{i,j}}\prod^{\to}_{i\in\mathsf{K}}Z_{i}^{\mathbf{a}_{i}},

where
>
denotes a total order on
\mathsf{K}
. Note that
Z^{\mathbf{a}}

does not depend on the choice of a total order. The set
\{Z^{\mathbf{a}}\ |\ \mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\oplus K}\}
forms an
\mathbb{A}
-basis of
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda)
. Since
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda)

is an Ore domain, it is embedded into the skew field of fraction
\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{T}(\Lambda))
.

Definition 2.2
.

We call a
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
-valued matrix
\widetilde{B}=(b_{i,j})_{i\in\mathsf{K},j\in\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{ex}}}

an
exchange
matrix if it satisfies the following properties:

(a)

For each
j\in\mathsf{K}
, there exists finitely many
i\in\mathsf{K}

such that
b_{i,j}\neq 0
.

(b)

Its principal part
B=(b_{i,j})_{i,j\in\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{ex}}}
is
skew-symmetrizable
with
an
\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}_{0}
-valued diagonal matrix
D
; i.e.,
DB
is skew-symmetric.

For an exchange matrix
\widetilde{B}
, we associate a
valued quiver

\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\widetilde{B}}
whose set of vertices is
\mathsf{K}
and arrows
between vertices are assigned by the following rules:

(2.2)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 2.71007pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-2.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.22223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bullet}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.7603pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\ulcorner}a,b{\lrcorner}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-2.71007pt\raise-5.43054pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{k}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 23.33325pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 23.33325pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.22223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bullet}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.04852pt\raise-5.43054pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{l}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces&\text{ if $l,k\in\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{ex}}$, $l\neq k$, $b_{kl}=a\geqslant 0$ and $b_{lk}=b\leqslant 0$},\\
\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 2.71007pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-2.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.22223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\circ}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.52556pt\raise 6.09166pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.73056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\ulcorner}a,0{\lrcorner}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-2.71007pt\raise-5.43054pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{k}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 23.33325pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 23.33325pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.22223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bullet}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.04852pt\raise-5.43054pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{l}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ \ \text{(resp.}\ \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 2.71007pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-2.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.22223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\circ}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 2.50002pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.93605pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\ulcorner}0,b{\lrcorner}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-2.71007pt\raise-5.43054pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{k}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 23.33325pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.22223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bullet}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.04852pt\raise-5.43054pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{l}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\text{)}&\text{ if $l\in\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{ex}}$, $k\in\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{fr}}$ and $b_{kl}=a\geqslant 0$ (resp. $b_{kl}=b\leqslant 0$)}.\end{cases}

Here we do not draw an arrow between
k
and
l
if
b_{kl}=0
(and
b_{lk}=0
when
l,k\in\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{ex}}
).
Note that
\circ
denotes a vertex in
\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{fr}}
, and
we call
\ulcorner a,b\lrcorner
the
value
of the arrow.

Convention 1
.

In this paper, we adopt the following scheme for depiction of a valued quiver:

(i)

if
b_{kl}=1
and
b_{lk}=-b<0
, use
\textstyle{k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{<b\;}
\textstyle{l}
,

(ii)

if
b_{kl}=2
and
b_{lk}=-b<0
, use
\textstyle{k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{<b\;}
\textstyle{l}
,

(iii)

if
b_{kl}=3
and
b_{lk}=-b<0
, use
\textstyle{k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{<b\;}
\textstyle{l\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

,

(iv)

we usually skip
<\hskip-3.01385pt1
in an arrow
(
when
\ulcorner a,-1\lrcorner
and
1\leqslant a\leqslant 3)
for notational simplicity,

(v)

if
b_{kl}=-b_{lk}=\alpha>1
, use
\textstyle{k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{l}
.

We say that a pair
(\Lambda,\widetilde{B})
is
compatible
if

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathsf{K}}b_{ki}\Lambda_{kj}=d_{i}\delta_{i,j}\ \text{for any }i\in\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{ex}},\ j\in\mathsf{K}\text{ and some positive integers $\{d_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{ex}}}$.}

In this case,

B
is skew-symmetrizable with the diagonal matrix
D=\mathrm{diag}(d_{i}\ |\ i\in\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{ex}})
.
Also, for
k\in\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{ex}}
, the
mutation
of
(\Lambda,\widetilde{B})

in direction
k
is a compatible pair

\mu_{k}(\Lambda,\widetilde{B})\mathbin{:=}(\mu_{k}(\Lambda),\mu_{k}(\widetilde{B}))
defined as follows (
[
2
]
):

\displaystyle\mu_{k}(\Lambda)\mathbin{:=}E^{T}\Lambda E\quad\text{ and }\quad\mu_{k}(\widetilde{B})\mathbin{:=}E\widetilde{B}F,

where the matrices
E=(e_{i,j})_{i,j\in\mathsf{K}}
and
F=(f_{i,j})_{i,j\in\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{ex}}}

are given by

\displaystyle e_{i,j}\mathbin{:=}\begin{cases}\;\delta_{i,j}&\text{ if }j\neq k,\\
-1&\text{ if }i=j=k,\\
[-b_{ik}]_{+}&\text{ if }i\neq j=k,\end{cases}\qquad f_{i,j}\mathbin{:=}\begin{cases}\;\delta_{i,j}&\text{ if }i\neq k,\\
-1&\text{ if }i=j=k,\\
\;[b_{kj}]_{+}&\text{ if }i=k\neq j.\end{cases}

Note that the mutation
\mu_{k}
is involutive; i.e.,
\mu_{k}(\mu_{k}(\Lambda,\widetilde{B}))=(\Lambda,\widetilde{B})
.
The mutation
\mu_{k}
on
\widetilde{B}
can be described as the operation
\mu_{k}
on its corresponding valued quiver
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\widetilde{B}}
in the following algorithm:

Algorithm 2.3
.

For
k\in\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{ex}}
, the
valued quiver mutation
\mu_{k}
transforms
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\widetilde{B}}
into a new valued quiver
\mu_{k}(\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\widetilde{B}})=\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\mu_{k}(\widetilde{B})}
via the following rules, where we assume
(i)

ac>0
or
bd>0
, and
(ii)
we do not perform
(
\mathcal{NC}
)
and
(
\mathcal{C}
)
below, if
i
and
j
are frozen at the same time
:

(
\mathcal{NC}
)
For each full-subquiver
\textstyle{i\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ulcorner a,b\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\ulcorner e,f\lrcorner}
\textstyle{k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ulcorner c,d\lrcorner}
\textstyle{j}
in
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\widetilde{B}}
,
we change the value of the arrow from
i
to
j
into
\ulcorner e+ac,f-bd\lrcorner:

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 7.64651pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.72256pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{i\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.64651pt\raise-6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ulcorner e+ac,f-bd\lrcorner}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.72256pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 28.72256pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{j}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

(
\mathcal{C}
)

For each full-subquiver
\textstyle{i\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ulcorner a,b\lrcorner}
\textstyle{k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ulcorner c,d\lrcorner}
\textstyle{j\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ulcorner e,f\lrcorner}
with
(e,f)\neq(0,0)
in
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\widetilde{B}}
, we change the valued arrow between
i
and
j
as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 7.64651pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.72256pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{i\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.72256pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.64651pt\raise-6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ulcorner e-bd,f+ac\lrcorner}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 28.72256pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{j}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces&\text{ if }f+ac\leqslant 0\leqslant e-bd,\\
\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 7.64651pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.72256pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{i\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.64651pt\raise-6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ulcorner f+ac,e-bd\lrcorner}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.72256pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 28.72256pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{j}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces&\text{ if }f+ac\geqslant 0\geqslant e-bd.\end{cases}

(
\mathcal{R}
)

Reverse the direction of each arrow incident to vertex
k
and change the value
\ulcorner a,b\lrcorner
of each arrow into
\ulcorner-b,-a\lrcorner
.

Here if there is no arrow between
i
and
j
in
(
\mathcal{NC}
)
and
(
\mathcal{C}
)
, then put
e=f=0
and follow the same rule.

We define an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Q}(q^{1/2})
-algebras

\mu_{k}^{*}:\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{T}(\mu_{k}\Lambda))\simeq\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{T}(\Lambda))
by

\mu_{k}^{*}(Z_{j})\mathbin{:=}\begin{cases}Z^{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}+Z^{\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}}&\text{ if }j=k,\\
Z_{j}&\text{ if }j\neq k,\end{cases}

where

\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime}=-\delta_{i,k}+\delta(i\neq k)[b_{ik}]_{+}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime\prime}=-\delta_{i,k}+\delta(i\neq k)[-b_{ik}]_{+}\quad\text{ for all }i\in\mathsf{K}.

Definition 2.4
.

For a compatible pair
(\Lambda,\widetilde{B})
, an element
x
in
\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{T}(\Lambda))
is called
a
quantum cluster variable
(resp.
quantum cluster monomial
)
if

\mathsf{z}=\mu^{*}_{k_{l}}\cdots\mu^{*}_{k_{2}}\mu^{*}_{k_{1}}(Z_{j})\qquad\text{(resp. }\mu^{*}_{k_{l}}\cdots\mu^{*}_{k_{2}}\mu^{*}_{k_{1}}(Z^{\mathbf{a}})\text{)},

for some finite sequence
(k_{1},k_{2},\ldots,k_{l})
in
\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{ex}}

and
j\in\mathsf{K}
(resp.
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}^{\oplus\mathsf{K}}
). The
quantum cluster algebra

\mathscr{A}_{q}(\Lambda,\widetilde{B})
is the
\mathbb{A}
-subalgebra of
\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{T}(\Lambda))

generated by all the quantum cluster variables. We call the triple
\mathcal{S}\mathbin{:=}(\{Z_{k}\}_{k\in\mathsf{K}},\Lambda,\widetilde{B})
the
initial seed
of
\mathscr{A}_{q}(\Lambda,\widetilde{B})
.

Theorem 2.5

(The quantum Laurent phenomenon
[
2
]
)
.

The quantum cluster algebra

\mathscr{A}_{q}(\Lambda,\widetilde{B})
is contained in
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda)
.

Remark 2.6
.

For a permutation
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mathsf{K}}
of
\mathsf{K}
preserving
\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{fr}}
, we
denote by
\pi(\Lambda,\widetilde{B})=(\pi\Lambda,\pi\widetilde{B})
where

(\pi\Lambda)_{i,j}\mathbin{:=}\Lambda_{\pi^{-1}(i)\pi^{-1}(j)}
for
i,j\in\mathsf{K}

and

(\pi\widetilde{B})_{i,j}\mathbin{:=}b_{\pi^{-1}(i)\pi^{-1}(j)}
for
(i,j)\in\mathsf{K}\times\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{ex}}
. The operation
\pi
on a compatible pair
(\Lambda,\widetilde{B})
obviously induces
the
\mathbb{A}
-algebra isomorphism

\pi^{*}:\mathscr{A}_{q}(\pi(\Lambda,\widetilde{B}))\simeq\mathscr{A}_{q}(\Lambda,\widetilde{B})\quad\text{ given by }\pi^{*}(Z_{j})=Z_{\pi^{-1}(j)}\text{ for all }j\in\mathsf{K}

and bijections between the sets of quantum cluster monomials.

Let
(\Lambda,\widetilde{B})
be a compatible pair. An element
\mathsf{z}
in
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda)
is
pointed

if it is of the following form:

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathsf{z}=q^{a}Z^{{\bm{g}}^{R}}+\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}^{\oplus\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{ex}}}\setminus\{0\}}p_{\bm{n}}Z^{{\bm{g}}^{R}+\widetilde{B}{\bm{n}}}

for some
a\in\dfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
,
{\bm{g}}^{R}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\oplus\mathsf{K}}
and
p_{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{A}
. In this case, we call
{\bm{g}}^{R}
the
degree
of the pointed
element
\mathsf{z}
and denote it by
\mathbf{deg}(\mathsf{z})
. Note that the notion of degree
does depend
on
\widetilde{B}
.

Theorem 2.7
.

[
7
,
5
,
17
,
53
]

(a)

Every quantum cluster monomial
\mathsf{z}
in
\mathscr{A}_{q}(\Lambda,\widetilde{B})
is pointed.

(b)

If two quantum cluster monomials
\mathsf{z},\mathsf{z}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{A}_{q}(\Lambda,\widetilde{B})
have the same degree, then
\mathsf{z}=\mathsf{z}^{\prime}
.

Let
\mathsf{z}\in\mathscr{A}_{q}(\Lambda,\widetilde{B})
and

\mathsf{z}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{A}_{q}(\mu_{k}(\Lambda,\widetilde{B}))
be pointed elements such that

\mathbf{deg}(\mathsf{z})=({\bm{g}}_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\oplus\mathsf{K}}

and

\mathbf{deg}(\mathsf{z}^{\prime})=({\bm{g}}^{\prime}_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\oplus\mathsf{K}}
.
If
\mu^{*}_{k}(\mathsf{z}^{\prime})=\mathsf{z}
, we have (
[
7
,
5
,
17
]
)

(2.4)

\displaystyle{\bm{g}}_{j}=\begin{cases}-{\bm{g}}^{\prime}_{k}&\text{ if }j=k,\\
{\bm{g}}^{\prime}_{j}+[b^{\prime}_{jk}]_{+}{\bm{g}}^{\prime}_{k}&\text{ if $j\neq k$ and ${\bm{g}}_{k}^{\prime}\geqslant 0$},\\
{\bm{g}}^{\prime}_{j}+[-b^{\prime}_{jk}]_{+}{\bm{g}}^{\prime}_{k}&\text{ if $j\neq k$ and ${\bm{g}}_{k}^{\prime}\leqslant 0$}.\end{cases}

3.
Behaviours of degrees with respect to braid moves and forward shifts

In this section, we observe the behaviours of degrees
with respect to several moves among sequences of simple reflections. For
2
-moves,
3
-moves and forward shifts, they are investigated in
[
11
]
. We mainly focus on the
4
-moves and
6
-moves, which are not
investigated before as far as authors know, and quite complicated. We mainly follow notations and frameworks in
[
11
]
.

3.1.
Setup

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a complex finite-dimensional simple Lie algebra.
We denote by
I
the index set of simple (co)roots
\Pi=\{{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
(resp.
\Pi^{\vee}=\{h_{i}\}_{i\in I}
), by
\triangle

the Dynkin diagram of
\mathfrak{g}
, by
\mathsf{h}
the Coxeter number of
\mathfrak{g}
,
by
\mathsf{C}=(\mathsf{c}_{i,j})_{i,j\in I}
the Cartan matrix of
\mathfrak{g}
,
by
\mathsf{D}={\rm diag}(\mathsf{d}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 1}\ |\ i\in I)
the minimal (left) symmetrizer of
\mathsf{C}
, by
\Phi^{\pm}
the set of positive (resp. negative) roots of
\mathfrak{g}
,
and by
\mathsf{P}
the weight lattice of
\mathfrak{g}
.
We write
i\sim j
for
i,j\in I
if
\mathsf{c}_{i,j}<0
,
and let

\varpi_{i}
be the fundamental weight for each
i\in I
.
We set

\mathsf{Q}\mathbin{:=}\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i}

and

\mathsf{Q}^{\pm}\mathbin{:=}\pm\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}_{0}{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i}
. For
{\mspace{1mu}\beta}=\sum_{i\in I}n_{i}{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i}\in\mathsf{Q}^{+}
, we set
|{\mspace{1mu}\beta}|\mathbin{:=}\sum_{i\in I}|n_{i}|
.
We denote by

d(i,j)
the number of edges between
i
and
j
in
\triangle
(see
[
26
]
for examples of
d(i,j)
).

We take the symmetric bilinear form
(-,-)
on
\mathsf{P}
such that

({\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{j})=\mathsf{d}_{i}\mathsf{c}_{i,j}=\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{c}_{j,i}\quad\text{ and }\quad\langle h_{i},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{j}\rangle=\dfrac{2({\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{j})}{({\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i})}=\mathsf{c}_{i,j}.

Let
\mathsf{W}
be the Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}
generated by simple reflections

\{s_{i}\}_{i\in I}
such that
s_{i}(\lambda)=\lambda-\langle h_{i},\lambda\rangle{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i}
for
\lambda\in\mathsf{P}
.
For a finite sequence
\textbf{{i}}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})
and a Weyl group
\mathsf{W}
,
we call
i

reduced
, if
s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{r}}
is a reduced expression of some
w
in
\mathsf{W}
. Note that there exists a unique longest element

w_{\circ}
in
\mathsf{W}
and

w_{\circ}
induces the automorphism
*
on
I
by
w_{\circ}({\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i})=-{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i^{*}}
.

We say that two sequences
\textbf{{i}}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})
and
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}=(i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{r})
of indices are
commutative equivalent
, denote by
\textbf{{i}}\overset{c}{\sim}\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}
, if
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}
can be obtained from
i

by applying 2-moves (or commutation moves)
(i_{k},i_{k+1})\to(i_{k+1}=i_{k}^{\prime},i_{k}=i^{\prime}_{k+1})
for
1\leqslant k<r
and
d(i_{k},i_{k+1})>1
. We denote by
[\textbf{{i}}]
the equivalence class of
i
under
\overset{c}{\sim}
.

Let
\textbf{{i}}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell})
be a reduced sequence of
w_{\circ}
. Then it is well-known
that

(3.1)

\displaystyle\Phi^{+}=\{{\mspace{1mu}\beta}^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k}\mathbin{:=}s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{k-1}}({\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i_{k}})\ |\ 1\leqslant k\leqslant\ell\}\quad\text{ and }\quad|\Phi^{+}|=\ell.

Example 3.1
.

For
\mathfrak{g}
of type
B_{2}
, there are only two reduced sequences
i
and
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}
of

w_{\circ}
given by
\textbf{{i}}=(1,2,1,2)
and
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}=(2,1,2,1)
, respectively.
Then

\displaystyle\Phi^{+}

\displaystyle=\{{\mspace{1mu}\beta}^{\textbf{{i}}}_{1}={\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{1},{\mspace{1mu}\beta}^{\textbf{{i}}}_{2}={\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{1}+{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{2},{\mspace{1mu}\beta}^{\textbf{{i}}}_{3}={\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{1}+2{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{2},{\mspace{1mu}\beta}^{\textbf{{i}}}_{4}={\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{2}\},

\displaystyle=\{{\mspace{1mu}\beta}^{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}_{1}={\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{2},{\mspace{1mu}\beta}^{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}_{2}={\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{1}+2{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{2},{\mspace{1mu}\beta}^{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}_{3}={\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{1}+{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{2},{\mspace{1mu}\beta}^{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}_{4}={\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{1}\}.

3.2.
Sequences of indices

Let
\textbf{{i}}=(i_{u})_{u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}\in I^{\mathbb{N}}\mspace{1mu}
be an arbitrary sequence such that

(3.2)

\displaystyle|\{u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}\ |\ i_{u}=i\}|=\infty\quad\text{ for any }i\in I.

We denote by
I^{(\infty)}
the set of sequences in
I^{\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}

satisfying (
3.2
).

For
\textbf{{i}}\in I^{r}
(
r\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}\sqcup\{\infty\}
),
u\in[1,r]
and
j\in I
, we set

\displaystyle u_{\textbf{{i}}}^{+}(j)

\displaystyle\mathbin{:=}\min(\{k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}\ |\ k>u,\ i_{k}=j\}\sqcup\{r+1\}),

\displaystyle u_{\textbf{{i}}}^{+}\mathbin{:=}u_{\textbf{{i}}}^{+}(i_{u}),

\displaystyle u_{\textbf{{i}}}^{-}(j)

\displaystyle\mathbin{:=}\max(\{k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}\ |\ k<u,\ i_{k}=j\}\sqcup\{0\}),

\displaystyle u_{\textbf{{i}}}^{-}\mathbin{:=}u_{\textbf{{i}}}^{-}(i_{u}),

\displaystyle u_{\textbf{{i}}}^{\min}(j)

\displaystyle\mathbin{:=}\min(k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}\ |\ \ i_{k}=j),

\displaystyle u_{\textbf{{i}}}^{\min}\mathbin{:=}u_{\textbf{{i}}}^{\min}(i_{u}).

We drop
{}_{\textbf{{i}}}
if there is no danger of confusion.

For
\textbf{{i}}\in I^{(\infty)}
, we define an
\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}\times\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
exchange matrix
\widetilde{B}^{\textbf{{i}}}=(b^{\textbf{{i}}}_{u,v})_{u,v\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
as follows:

b^{\textbf{{i}}}_{u,v}\mathbin{:=}\begin{cases}\pm 1&\text{ if }v=u^{\pm},\\
\pm\mathsf{c}_{i_{u},i_{v}}&\text{ if }u<v<u^{+}<v^{+}\text{ (resp. }v<u<v^{+}<u^{+}\text{)},\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Note that
\widetilde{B}^{\textbf{{i}}}
is skew-symmetrizable with
\mathrm{diag}(\mathsf{d}_{i_{u}}\ |\ u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu})
and satisfies the conditions in Definition
2.2
.

Let
\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}}=(\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}}_{u,v})
be a skew-symmetric matrix given by

\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}}_{u,v}=-\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}}_{v,u}\mathbin{:=}(\varpi_{i_{u}}-w^{\textbf{{i}}}_{u}\varpi_{i_{u}},\varpi_{i_{v}}+w^{\textbf{{i}}}_{v}\varpi_{i_{v}})\quad\text{ for }u\leqslant v,

where

w^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k}\mathbin{:=}s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{k}}\in\mathsf{W}\quad\text{ for }k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}.

For
s\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
, we set

\mathsf{K}\mathbin{:=}[1,s],\qquad\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{fr}}\mathbin{:=}\{k\in\mathsf{K}\ |\ k^{+}>s\},\qquad\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{ex}}\mathbin{:=}\mathsf{K}\setminus\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{fr}}.

Lemma 3.2
.

[
11
, Lemma 1.1]

For
u,v\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
with
u<v^{+}
, we have
\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}}_{u,v}=(\varpi_{i_{u}}-w^{\textbf{{i}}}_{u}\varpi_{i_{u}},\varpi_{i_{v}}+w^{\textbf{{i}}}_{v}\varpi_{i_{v}}).

Proposition 3.3
.

[
11
, Proposition 1.2]

Let
\textbf{{i}}\in I^{(\infty)}
.

(a)

The pair
(\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}},\widetilde{B}^{\textbf{{i}}})
is
compatible with
\mathrm{diag}(\mathsf{d}_{i_{u}})_{u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
; i.e., for any
u,v\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
, we have

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}b^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k,u}\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k,v}=2\mathsf{d}_{i_{u}}\delta_{u,v}.

(b)

For each
s\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
, we set

\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}},s}\mathbin{:=}(\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}}_{u,v})_{u,v\in\mathsf{K}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{B}^{\textbf{{i}},s}\mathbin{:=}(b^{\textbf{{i}}}_{u,v})_{u\in\mathsf{K},v\in\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{ex}}}.

Then the pair
(\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}},s},\widetilde{B}^{\textbf{{i}},s})
is compatible with
\mathrm{diag}(\mathsf{d}_{i_{k}})_{k\in\mathsf{K}_{\mathrm{ex}}}
.

We define
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\textbf{{i}}}=(\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\textbf{{i}}}_{0},\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\textbf{{i}}}_{1})
as the valued quiver associated with

\widetilde{B}^{\textbf{{i}}}
, where
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\textbf{{i}}}_{0}
denotes the set of vertices and
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\textbf{{i}}}_{1}
denotes the
set of arrows. For a subset
\mathcal{K}
of
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\textbf{{i}}}_{0}
, we denote by
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\textbf{{i}}}|_{\mathcal{K}}

the full subquiver of
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\textbf{{i}}}
whose set of vertices is
\mathcal{K}
.

We denote by
\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}}
the quantum cluster algebra associated with the compatible pair
(\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}},\widetilde{B}^{\textbf{{i}}})
, which is an
\mathbb{A}
-subalgebra
of the quantum torus
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}})
. For each
s\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
, we also have the quantum cluster algebra
\mathcal{A}^{s}_{\textbf{{i}}}=\mathscr{A}_{q}(\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}},s},\widetilde{B}^{\textbf{{i}},s})

in the quantum torus
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}},s})
. Then we have

\raisebox{15.00002pt}{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 18.4792pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.99307pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 8.99307pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.1875pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-7.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-12.818pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-18.4792pt\raise-21.65526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 18.4792pt\raise-21.65526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 35.70137pt\raise-21.65526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 35.70137pt\raise-21.65526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{T}(\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}},2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 72.65977pt\raise-21.65526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 89.88194pt\raise-21.65526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 89.88194pt\raise-21.65526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 103.38194pt\raise-21.65526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 120.60411pt\raise-21.65526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 120.60411pt\raise-21.65526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{T}(\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\quad\text{ and }\quad\bigcup_{s\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}}^{s}=\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}}.

For
\textbf{{i}}\in I^{\mathbb{N}}\mspace{1mu}
, let
C_{\textbf{{i}}}\subset\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
denote the cone defined by

(3.3)

C_{\textbf{{i}}}\mathbin{:=}\left\{{\bm{g}}=(g_{u})_{u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}\;\middle|\;\sum_{v\geqslant u,i_{v}=i_{u}}g_{v}\geqslant 0,\forall u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}\right\}=\sum_{u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}_{0}({\bm{e}}_{u}-{\bm{e}}_{u^{-}_{\textbf{{i}}}}),

where
\{{\bm{e}}_{u}\}_{u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
is the natural basis of
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
and we understand
{\bm{e}}_{0}=0
.

For
{\bm{g}}=(g_{u})_{u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
and
\textbf{{i}}\in I^{\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
, write

(3.4)

\mathsf{p}_{\textbf{{i}}}({\bm{g}};i)\mathbin{:=}\sum_{u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu},i_{u}=i}g_{u}.

3.3.
2
-moves

In this and next subsections, we recall the results on

2
-moves and
3
-moves which are investigated in
[
11
]
.

For sequences
\textbf{{i}}=(i_{u})_{u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}},\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}=(i^{\prime}_{u})_{u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}\in I^{(\infty)}
,
we say that
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}
can be obtained from
i
via a
2
-move
, if there
exists a unique
k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
such that

i_{k}=i^{\prime}_{k+1},\ \ i_{k+1}=i^{\prime}_{k},\ \ i_{u}=i^{\prime}_{u}\ \ (u\not\in\{k,k+1\})

and

\mathsf{c}_{i_{k+1},i_{k}}=\mathsf{c}_{i_{k},i_{k+1}}=0.

In this case, we write
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}=\gamma_{k}\textbf{{i}}
.

Proposition 3.4
.

[
11
, §2.1]

Let
\textbf{{i}},\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}\in I^{\mathbb{N}}\mspace{1mu}
be related by
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}=\gamma_{k}\textbf{{i}}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
.

(i)

\widetilde{B}^{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}=\sigma_{k}\widetilde{B}^{\textbf{{i}}}
and
\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}=\sigma_{k}\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}}
.

(ii)

We have an
\mathbb{A}
-algebra isomorphism

\gamma^{*}_{k}(=\sigma^{*}_{k}):\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}\mathop{\xrightarrow[\raisebox{0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.90417pt]{$\scriptstyle{}$}]{{\raisebox{-1.80835pt}[0.0pt][-1.80835pt]{$\mspace{2mu}\sim\mspace{2mu}$}}}}\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}}\quad\text{ given by }Z_{u}\mapsto Z_{\sigma_{k}(u)}\text{ for all }u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu},

which induces a bijection between the sets of quantum cluster monomials.

(iii)

If
\mathsf{z}\in\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}
is a quantum cluster monomial with
\mathbf{deg}(\mathsf{z})={\bm{g}}^{\prime}=(g^{\prime}_{u})_{u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
, then the quantum cluster monomial

\gamma_{k}^{*}\mathsf{z}\in\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}}
has
\mathbf{deg}(\gamma_{k}^{*}\mathsf{z})={\bm{g}}=(g_{u})_{u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
as follows:

(3.5)

\displaystyle g_{u}=\begin{cases}g^{\prime}_{\sigma_{k}(u)}&\text{ if }u\in\{k,k+1\},\\
g^{\prime}_{u}&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

(iv)

The map
{\bm{g}}^{\prime}\mapsto{\bm{g}}
given by (
3.5
) sends the cone
C_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}\subset\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
into the cone
C_{\textbf{{i}}}\subset\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
.

(v)

The elements
{\bm{g}},{\bm{g}}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
related by (
3.5
) satisfy

\mathsf{p}_{\textbf{{i}}}({\bm{g}};i)=\mathsf{p}_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}({\bm{g}}^{\prime};i)\quad\text{ for all $i\in I$.}

3.4.
3
-moves

For sequences
\textbf{{i}}=(i_{u})_{u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}},\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}=(i^{\prime}_{u})_{u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}\in I^{\infty}
,
we say that
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}
can be obtained from
i
via a
3
-move
, if there
exists a unique
k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
such that

i_{k}=i_{k+2}=i^{\prime}_{k+1},\ \ i_{k+1}=i^{\prime}_{k}=i^{\prime}_{k+2},\ \ i_{u}=i^{\prime}_{u}\ \ (u\not\in[k,k+2])

and

\mathsf{c}_{i_{k+1},i_{k}}\mathsf{c}_{i_{k},i_{k+1}}=1.

In this case, we write
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}=\beta_{k}\textbf{{i}}
.

Proposition 3.5
.

[
11
, §2.2]

Let
\textbf{{i}},\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}\in I^{(\infty)}
be related by
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}=\beta_{k}\textbf{{i}}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
.

(i)

\widetilde{B}^{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}=\sigma_{k+1}\mu_{k}\widetilde{B}^{\textbf{{i}}}
and
\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}=\sigma_{k+1}\mu_{k}\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}}
.

(ii)

We have an
\mathbb{A}
-algebra isomorphism

\beta^{*}_{k}(=\mu^{*}_{k}\sigma^{*}_{k+1}):\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}\mathop{\xrightarrow[\raisebox{0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.90417pt]{$\scriptstyle{}$}]{{\raisebox{-1.80835pt}[0.0pt][-1.80835pt]{$\mspace{2mu}\sim\mspace{2mu}$}}}}\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}}\quad\text{ given by }Z_{u}\mapsto\mu_{k}(Z_{\sigma_{k+1}(u)})\text{ for all }u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu},

which induces a bijection between the sets of quantum cluster monomials.

(iii)

If
\mathsf{z}\in\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}
is a quantum cluster monomial with
\mathbf{deg}(\mathsf{z})={\bm{g}}^{\prime}=(g^{\prime}_{u})_{u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
, then the quantum cluster monomial

\beta_{k}^{*}\mathsf{z}\in\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}}
has
\mathbf{deg}(\beta_{k}^{*}\mathsf{z})={\bm{g}}=(g_{u})_{u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
as follows:

(3.6)

\displaystyle g_{u}=\begin{cases}-g^{\prime}_{k}&\text{ if }u=k,\\
g^{\prime}_{\sigma_{k+1}(u)}+[g_{k}^{\prime}]_{+}&\text{ if }u\in\{k+2,(k+1)_{\textbf{{i}}}^{-}\},\\
g^{\prime}_{\sigma_{k+1}(u)}-[-g_{k}^{\prime}]_{+}&\text{ if }u\in\{k+1,k_{\textbf{{i}}}^{-}\},\\
g^{\prime}_{u}&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

(iv)

The map
{\bm{g}}^{\prime}\mapsto{\bm{g}}
given by (
3.6
) sends the cone
C_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}\subset\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
into the cone
C_{\textbf{{i}}}\subset\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
.

(v)

The elements
{\bm{g}},{\bm{g}}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
related by (
3.6
) satisfy

\mathsf{p}_{\textbf{{i}}}({\bm{g}};i)=\begin{cases}\mathsf{p}_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}({\bm{g}}^{\prime};i)+[-g_{k}^{\prime}]_{+}&\text{ if }i=i_{k}\text{ and }k^{-}_{\textbf{{i}}}=0,\\
\mathsf{p}_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}({\bm{g}}^{\prime};i)-[g_{k}^{\prime}]_{+}&\text{ if }i=i_{k+1}\text{ and }(k+1)^{-}_{\textbf{{i}}}=0,\\
\mathsf{p}_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}({\bm{g}}^{\prime};i)&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

3.5.
4
-moves

For sequences
\textbf{{i}}=(i_{u})_{u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}},\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}=(i^{\prime}_{u})_{u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}\in I^{(\infty)}
,
we say that
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}
can be obtained from
i
via a
4
-move
, if there
exists a unique
k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
such that

i_{k}=i_{k+2}=i^{\prime}_{k+1}=i^{\prime}_{k+3},\ \ i_{k+1}=i_{k+3}=i^{\prime}_{k}=i^{\prime}_{k+2},\ \ i_{u}=i^{\prime}_{u}\ \ (u\not\in[k,k+3])

and

\mathsf{c}_{i_{k+1},i_{k}}\mathsf{c}_{i_{k},i_{k+1}}=2.

In this case, we write
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}=\eta_{k}\textbf{{i}}
.
Throughout this subsection, we assume that
\textbf{{i}},\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}\in I^{\mathbb{N}}\mspace{1mu}
are related by
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}=\eta_{k}\textbf{{i}}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
and sometimes write
i\mathbin{:=}i_{k}
and
j\mathbin{:=}i_{k+1}
.

Lemma 3.6
.

We have

(3.7)

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}^{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}=\sigma_{k+2}\sigma_{k}\mu_{k}\mu_{k+1}\mu_{k}\widetilde{B}^{\textbf{{i}}}.

Proof.

By condition (
a
) in Definition
2.2
,
it is enough to consider the local behaviour under the operation in (
3.7
).
Note that

(1)

the set
\mathcal{K}=\{k,k+1,k+2,k+3,k^{-}_{\textbf{{i}}}=(k+1)^{-}_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}},(k+1)^{-}_{\textbf{{i}}}=k^{-}_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}\}

consists of vertices adjacent to
\{k,k+1\}
in
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\textbf{{i}}}
(resp.
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}
) and themselves,

(2)

the sequence of mutations
\mu_{k}\mu_{k+1}\mu_{k}
on
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\textbf{{i}}}
only affects the full subquiver
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\textbf{{i}}}|_{\mathcal{K}}
(resp.
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}|_{\mathcal{K}}
).

Thus it is enough to check that

\mu_{k}\mu_{k+1}\mu_{k}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\textbf{{i}}}|_{\mathcal{K}}=\sigma_{k}\sigma_{k+2}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}|_{\mathcal{K}}.

In this proof,
we shall only consider the case when both
k^{-}_{\textbf{{i}}}
and
(k+1)^{-}_{\textbf{{i}}}
exist, since the proofs for the other cases are similar. In that case, the valued quiver
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\textbf{{i}}}|_{\mathcal{K}}
can be depicted as follows:

.

Here

•

the dotted arrow between

and
