\scriptstyle{\varphi_{\textbf{{i}}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathfrak{n})}

Moreover, the isomorphism
{\bm{\tau}}^{*}=\varphi_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ\varphi_{\textbf{{i}}}
depends only on the pair
(\textbf{{i}},\textbf{{i}}^{\prime})
, and not on the sequence
{\bm{\tau}}
satisfying
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}=\tau_{1}\cdots\tau_{l}\textbf{{i}}
.

5.
Homomorphisms and substitution formulas

In this section, we first review the quantum (virtual) Grothendieck ring
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})

associated with
\mathfrak{g}
, bases of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})

and their subrings.
For
\mathfrak{g}
of simply-laced type, the ring
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is introduced in
[
48
,
54
,
18
]

while it is introduced in
[
39
,
26
,
25
]
for
\mathfrak{g}
of non-simply-laced type.
Then we study homomorphisms on subrings of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
and the skew field of fraction
\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}_{q})
, which are closely related to braid moves and the theory of quantum cluster algebras.

5.1.
Quantum torus
\mathcal{X}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
and the ring
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})

Let
t
be an indeterminate. For
i,j\in I
, we set

\underline{\mathsf{c}}_{i,j}(t)\mathbin{:=}\delta_{i,j}(t+t^{-1})+\delta(i\neq j)\mathsf{c}_{i,j}

and call the matrix
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(t)\mathbin{:=}(\underline{\mathsf{c}}_{i,j}(t))
the
t
-quantized Cartan matrix
. Then

\underline{\mathsf{B}}(t)\mathbin{:=}\underline{\mathsf{C}}(t)\mathsf{D}^{-1}\text{ is a symmetric matrix in }{\rm GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[t^{\pm 1}]).

Regarding
\underline{\mathsf{B}}(t)
as a
\mathbb{Q}(t)
-valued matrix, it is invertible and we denote the inverse of
\underline{\mathsf{B}}(t)
by

\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{\underline{\mathsf{B}}}(t)=(\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{\underline{\mathsf{B}}}_{i,j}(t))_{i,j\in I}
. Write

\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{\underline{\mathsf{B}}}_{i,j}(t)=\sum_{u\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}}\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(u)t^{u}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}(\!(t)\!)

for the Laurent expansion of
\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{\underline{\mathsf{B}}}_{i,j}(t)
at
t=0
. It is proved in

[
22
,
39
]
that,
for any
i,j\in I
and
u\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
, we have

(5.1)

\displaystyle\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(u)=0\quad\text{ if {\rm(i)} $u\leqslant d(i,j)$}\quad\text{ or }\quad\text{{\rm(ii)} $d(i,j)\equiv u$ $\pmod{2}$.}

We fix a parity function
\epsilon:I\to\{0,1\}
such that
\epsilon_{i}\neq\epsilon_{j}
for
i\sim j
, based on (
5.1
), and
define

(5.2)

\displaystyle{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}\mathbin{:=}\{(i,p)\in I\times\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}\ |\ p\equiv\epsilon_{i}\pmod{2}\}\subsetneq I\times\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}.

Definition 5.1

(
[
48
,
18
,
55
,
39
]
)
.

We define a quantum torus
(\mathcal{X}_{q}(\mathfrak{g}),*)
to be the
\mathbb{A}
-algebra generated by
\{\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{\pm}\ |\ (i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}\}
subject to the following relations:

\widetilde{X}_{i,p}*\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{-1}=\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{-1}*\widetilde{X}_{i,p}=1\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{X}_{i,p}*\widetilde{X}_{j,s}=q^{\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(i,p;j,s)}\widetilde{X}_{j,s}*\widetilde{X}_{i,p},

where

\displaystyle\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(i,p;j,s)=\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(p-s-1)-\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(s-p-1)-\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(p-s+1)+\mspace{1mu}\widetilde{{\underline{\mathsf{b}}}}_{i,j}(s-p+1).

Note that there exists a
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
-algebra anti-involution on
\mathcal{X}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
given by

\displaystyle q^{1/2}\longmapsto q^{-1/2}\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{X}_{i,p}\longmapsto q^{\mathsf{d}_{i}}\widetilde{X}_{i,p}.

We will write
\mathcal{X}_{q}
instead of
\mathcal{X}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
if there is no danger of confusion.
We denote by
\mathcal{X}
the commutative Laurent polynomial ring obtained from
\mathcal{X}_{q}
by specializing
q
at
1
. We write the map
{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}:\mathcal{X}_{q}\to\mathcal{X}

and set
X_{i,p}\mathbin{:=}{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}(q^{a/2}\widetilde{X}_{i,p})
for any
a\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
. We call a product
\widetilde{m}
of
\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{\pm 1}
and
q^{\pm 1/2}
in
\mathcal{X}_{q}
a
monomial
; i.e.,
it is of the form

\widetilde{m}=q^{a/2}\mathop{\mbox{\normalsize$*$}}\limits^{\to}_{(i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}}\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{u_{i,p}(\widetilde{m})}\quad\text{for }a,u_{i,p}(\widetilde{m})\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}.

We also call a monomial
\widetilde{m}\in\mathcal{X}_{q}

commutative
if
\widetilde{m}=\overline{\widetilde{m}}
, and
dominant
(resp.
anti-dominant
) if it is a product of
\widetilde{X}_{i,p}
(resp.
\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{-1}
) and
q^{\pm 1/2}
.

For a monomial
m\in\mathcal{X}
, we can associate the commutative monomial

\underline{m}\in\mathcal{X}_{q}
such that
{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}(\underline{m})=m
. Thus we write
\underline{m}
for commutative monomials in
\mathcal{X}_{q}
. Let
\mathcal{M}
be the set of all monomials in
\mathcal{X}
,
and
\mathcal{M}_{+}
be the set of all dominant monomials in
\mathcal{X}
.

For monomials
\widetilde{m},\widetilde{m}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{X}_{q}
, define

\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(\widetilde{m},\widetilde{m}^{\prime})\mathbin{:=}\sum_{(i,p),(j,s)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}}u_{i,p}(\widetilde{m})u_{j,s}(\widetilde{m}^{\prime})\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(i,p;j,s).

For each
(i,p)\in I\times\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
such that
(i,p\pm 1)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
, define the commutative monomial

\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}_{i,p}\mathbin{:=}{\underline{\mathscr{B}}}_{i,p},

where

\mathscr{B}_{i,p}=X_{i,p-1}X_{i,p+1}\prod_{j\sim i}X_{j,p}^{\mathsf{c}_{j,i}}\in\mathcal{X}
.

Definition 5.2

(
[
18
,
39
,
26
]
)
.

(a)

For each
i\in I
, denote by
\mathfrak{K}_{i,q}(\mathfrak{g})
the
\mathbb{A}
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{X}_{q}
generated by

\widetilde{X}_{i,p}*(1+q^{-\mathsf{d}_{i}}\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}_{i,p+1}^{-1})\ \text{ and }\ \widetilde{X}_{j,s}^{\pm 1}\quad\text{ for }j\in I\setminus\{i\}\text{ and }(i,p),(j,s)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}.

(b)

Define

\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\mathbin{:=}\bigcap_{i\in I}\mathfrak{K}_{i,q}(\mathfrak{g})

and call it the
quantum virtual Grothendieck ring
associated with
\underline{\mathsf{C}}(t)
. If there is no confusion, we write
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
instead of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
.

5.2.
Bases

For
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
, there exists a partial order
\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}
, called
Nakajima order
, defined by

m\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}m^{\prime}\text{ if and only if }m^{-1}m^{\prime}\text{ is a product of elements in }\{\mathscr{B}_{i,p}\ |\ (i,p\pm 1)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}\}.

For monomials
\widetilde{m},\widetilde{m}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{q}
, we write
\widetilde{m}\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}\widetilde{m}^{\prime}
if
{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}(\widetilde{m})\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}(\widetilde{m}^{\prime})
.

Theorem 5.3

(
[
18
,
26
]
, cf.
[
8
]
)
.

For each dominant monomial
m\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, there exists a unique element
F_{q}(m)
of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
such that

\underline{m}
is the unique dominant monomial appearing in
F_{q}(m)

and
\overline{F_{q}(m)}=F_{q}(m)
.

Furthermore,

(a)

a monomial appearing in
F_{q}(m)-\underline{m}
is strictly less than
\underline{m}
with respect to
\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}
,

(b)

\mathsf{F}_{q}\mathbin{:=}\{F_{q}(m)\ |\ m\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\}
forms an
\mathbb{A}
-basis of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
,

(c)

\mathfrak{K}_{q}
is generated by the set
\{F_{q}(X_{i,p})\ |\ (i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}\}
as an
\mathbb{A}
-algebra.

For
m\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, we set

E_{q}(m)\mathbin{:=}q^{b}\left(\mathop{\mbox{\normalsize$*$}}\limits^{\to}_{p\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}}\left(\mathop{\mbox{\normalsize$*$}}\limits_{i;\in I,(i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}}F_{q}(X_{i,p})^{u_{i,p}(m)}\right)\right),

where
b
is an element in
\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
such that
\underline{m}
appears in
E_{q}(m)
with coefficient
1
.
Then the set
\mathsf{E}_{q}\mathbin{:=}\{E_{q}(m)\ |\ m\in\mathcal{M}\}
also forms an
\mathbb{A}
-basis of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, called the
standard basis
, and satisfies

(5.3)

\displaystyle E_{q}(m)=F_{q}(m)+\sum_{m^{\prime}\prec_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}m}C_{m,m^{\prime}}F_{q}(m^{\prime})\quad\text{for some $C_{m,m^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{A}$.}

Theorem 5.4

(
[
48
,
18
,
26
]
)
.

For each
m\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, there exists a unique element
L_{q}(m)
in
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
such that

(5.4)

(i)

\overline{L_{q}(m)}=L_{q}(m)
and

(ii)

L_{q}(m)=E_{q}(m)+\displaystyle\sum_{m^{\prime}\prec_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}m}Q_{m,m^{\prime}}(q)E_{q}(m^{\prime})
for some
Q_{m,m^{\prime}}(q)\in q\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q]
.

Furthermore,

(a)

a monomial appearing in
L_{q}(m)-\underline{m}
is strictly less than
\underline{m}
with respect to
\preccurlyeq_{\mspace{-2mu}\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{\scalebox{.6}{${\rm N}$}}}
,

(b)

\mathsf{L}_{q}\mathbin{:=}\{L_{q}(m)\ |\ m\in\mathcal{M}\}
forms an
\mathbb{A}
-basis of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
called the
canonical basis
of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
,

(c)

L_{q}(X_{i,p})=F_{q}(X_{i,p})
for all
(i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}_{q}^{\pm 1}
be the
\mathbb{A}
-algebra automorphism of
\mathcal{X}_{q}

given by
\widetilde{X}_{i,p}\mapsto\widetilde{X}_{i^{*},p\pm\mathsf{h}}
, where
\mathsf{h}
is the Coxeter number of
\mathfrak{g}
. It is well-known that it
induces a
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
-algebra automorphism
\mathfrak{D}
on
\mathcal{X}
satisfying
{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}\circ\mathfrak{D}^{\pm 1}_{q}=\mathfrak{D}^{\pm 1}\circ{\operatorname{ev}}_{q=1}
and
preserves the subalgebra
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
of
\mathcal{X}_{q}
. Moreover, we have

(5.5)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{q}^{\pm 1}(F_{q}(m))=F_{q}(\mathfrak{D}^{\pm 1}m)\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathfrak{D}_{q}^{\pm 1}(L_{q}(m))=L_{q}(\mathfrak{D}^{\pm 1}m)\quad\text{ for any }m\in\mathcal{M}_{+}.

Remark 5.5
.

Note that, for
r\in 2\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
, there exists an automorphism
\mathsf{T}_{r}
of
\mathcal{X}_{q}
sending

\widetilde{X}_{i,p}
to
\widetilde{X}_{i,p+r}
satisfying the following properties:

(a)

The restriction to
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
gives an automorphism of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
.

(b)

For each
m\in\mathcal{M}^{+}
, we have
\mathsf{T}_{r}(F_{q}(m))=F_{q}(\mathtt{T}_{r}(m))
,
\mathsf{T}_{r}(E_{q}(m))=E_{q}(\mathtt{T}_{r}(m))
and
\mathsf{T}_{r}(L_{q}(m))=L_{q}(\mathtt{T}_{r}(m))
.
Here
\mathtt{T}_{r}
denotes the induced automorphism on
\mathcal{X}
.

5.3.
Kirillov–Reshetikhin and fundamental polynomials

For a pair
(i,p),(i,s)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
with
p\leqslant s
, we set

(5.6)

m^{(i)}[p,s]\mathbin{:=}\prod_{(i,u)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0},\ p\leqslant u\leqslant s}X_{i,s}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}.

We call
m^{(i)}[p,s]
the
Kirillov–Reshetikhin
(
KR
)
monomial
and

F_{q}(m^{(i)}[p,s])\in\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
the
KR-polynomial
for the pair
(i,p)
and
(i,s)
, respectively.
In particular, we call
F_{q}(X_{i,p})=F_{q}(m^{(i)}[p,p])
the
fundamental polynomial
for each
(i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
.

Theorem 5.6

(
[
47
,
20
,
10
]
)
.

For each KR-monomial
m^{(i)}[p,s]
, the KR-polynomial
F_{q}(m^{(i)}[p,s])
satisfies the following properties:

(a)

F_{q}(m^{(i)}[p,s])
contains the unique dominant monomial
\underline{m^{(i)}[p,s]}
.

(b)

F_{q}(m^{(i)}[p,s])
contains the unique anti-dominant monomial
\underline{m^{(i)}[p+\mathsf{h},s+\mathsf{h}]}
.

(c)

Each
\mathcal{X}_{q}
-monomial of
F_{q}(\underline{m^{(i)}[p,s]})-\underline{m^{(i)}[p,s]}-\underline{m^{(i^{*})}[p+\mathsf{h},s+\mathsf{h}]}
is a product of
\widetilde{X}_{j,u}^{\pm 1}
with
p\leqslant u\leqslant s+\mathsf{h}
, having at least one of its factors from
p<u<s+\mathsf{h}
. In particular, each monomial of
F_{q}(\underline{\widetilde{X}_{i,p}})-\underline{\widetilde{X}_{i,p}}-\underline{\widetilde{X}_{i^{*},p+\mathsf{h}}}
is a product of
\widetilde{X}_{j,u}^{\pm 1}
with
p<u<p+\mathsf{h}
.

5.4.
Height functions and their negative subrings

A
height function
\xi
on
\triangle
is a function
\xi:I\to\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
given by
i\mapsto\xi_{i}
satisfying

\xi_{i}\equiv\epsilon_{i}\pmod{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad|\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}|=1\quad\text{if }i\sim j.

For a height function
\xi
on
\triangle
, we set

{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}\mathbin{:=}\{(i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}\ |\ p\leqslant\xi_{i}\}.

We call a bijective function
\widetilde{{\bm{\rho}}}:\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}\to{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}

a compatible reading of
{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
if
k>l
when
\widetilde{{\bm{\rho}}}(k)=(i,p)
,
\widetilde{{\bm{\rho}}}(l)=(j,p+1)\in{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
and
i\sim j
. For a compatible reading
\widetilde{{\bm{\rho}}}
of
{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
, we set
{\bm{\rho}}:\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}\to I^{(\infty)}
where
{\bm{\rho}}(k)=i_{k}

for
\widetilde{{\bm{\rho}}}(k)=(i_{k},p_{k})\in{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
. We abuse
{\bm{\rho}}
to be the image
(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots)\in I^{\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}

of
\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
under
{\bm{\rho}}
and call it an
I
-compatible reading of
{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
.

For each
(i,p)\in{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widetilde{\triangle}}_{0}
, set the KR-monomial

Z^{\xi}_{i,p}\mathbin{:=}\underline{m^{(i)}[p,\xi_{i}]}\in\mathcal{X}_{q}.

When there is no danger of confusion, we write
Z_{i,p}
instead of

Z^{\xi}_{i,p}
for notational simplicity.
Denote by
\mathcal{X}_{q,\leqslant\xi}
the quantum subtorus of
\mathcal{X}_{q}
generated by

\{\widetilde{X}_{i,p}\}_{(i,p)\in{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.60275pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}}
.

Proposition 5.7

(
[
39
, Theorem 7.1]
, see also
[
3
, Proposition 5.1.1]
)
.

Let
{\bm{\rho}}=(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots)
and

{\bm{\rho}}^{\prime}=(j_{1},j_{2},\ldots)

be
I
-compatible readings of
{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
.

(i)

For
u\leqslant v
, we have

\mspace{1mu}\underline{\mathcal{N}}(Z^{\xi}_{i_{u},p_{u}},Z^{\xi}_{i_{v},p_{v}})=(\varpi_{i_{u}}-w^{\bm{\rho}}_{u}\varpi_{i_{u}},\varpi_{i_{v}}+w^{\bm{\rho}}_{u}\varpi_{i_{v}}),

where
\widetilde{{\bm{\rho}}}(u)=(i_{u},p_{u})
and
\widetilde{{\bm{\rho}}}(v)=(i_{v},p_{v})
. Hence we have an
\mathbb{A}
-algebra isomorphism

(5.7)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\kappa}_{\bm{\rho}}:\mathcal{T}(\Lambda^{{\bm{\rho}}})\mathop{\xrightarrow[\raisebox{0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.90417pt]{$\scriptstyle{}$}]{{\raisebox{-1.80835pt}[0.0pt][-1.80835pt]{$\mspace{2mu}\sim\mspace{2mu}$}}}}\mathcal{X}_{q,\leqslant\xi}\quad\text{ given by }\quad Z_{u}\longmapsto Z^{\xi}_{i_{u},p_{u}}=\underline{m^{(i_{u})}[p_{u},\xi_{i_{u}}]}.

(ii)

We have
{\bm{\rho}}\overset{c}{\sim}{\bm{\rho}}^{\prime}
and there exists

\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mathbb{N}}\mspace{1mu}
such that

(\Lambda^{{\bm{\rho}}},\widetilde{B}^{{\bm{\rho}}})=\pi(\Lambda^{{\bm{\rho}}^{\prime}},\widetilde{B}^{{\bm{\rho}}^{\prime}}).

For an element
\widetilde{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{q}
,
we denote by
\widetilde{x}_{\leqslant\xi}
the element of
\mathcal{X}_{q,\leqslant\xi}
obtained from
\widetilde{x}

by discarding all the monomials containing factors
\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{\pm 1}

with
(i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}\setminus{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
. We write the
\mathbb{A}
-linear map

\displaystyle(\cdot)_{\leqslant\xi}:\mathcal{X}_{q}\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{q,\leqslant\xi},\qquad\widetilde{x}\longmapsto\widetilde{x}_{\leqslant\xi}.

Definition 5.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi}
be the subring of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
generated by
\{F_{q}(X_{i,p})\ |\ (i,p)\in{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}\}
and call it the
\xi
-negative subring

of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
.

We set

(\Lambda^{\xi},\widetilde{B}^{\xi})\mathbin{:=}\left(\Lambda^{[{\bm{\rho}}]},\widetilde{B}^{[{\bm{\rho}}]}\right).

Remark 5.9
.

In
[
25
]
, the subring
\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi}
was written as
\mathfrak{K}_{q,\leqslant\xi}
. We hope there is no confusion by these notations.

Proposition 5.10

(
[
21
, Proposition 6.1]
,
[
26
, Proposition 6.3]
)
.

For a height function
\xi
on
\triangle
, the
\mathbb{A}
-linear map

(\cdot)_{\leqslant\xi}
induces the injective
\mathbb{A}
-algebra homomorphism

(5.8)

\displaystyle(\cdot)_{\leqslant\xi}:\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{q,\leqslant\xi}.

We call
(\cdot)_{\leqslant\xi}
in (
5.8
) the
truncation homomorphism
. Note that, for
a monomial
m^{(i)}[p,\xi_{i}]
with
p\leqslant\xi_{i}
and
p\equiv_{2}\xi_{i}
, we have

(5.9)

\displaystyle(F_{q}(m^{(i)}[p,\xi_{i}]))_{\leqslant\xi}=\underline{m^{(i)}[p,\xi_{i}]}

by Theorem
5.6
(
c
).

Proposition 5.11

(cf.
[
23
,
39
]
see also
[
11
, Proposition 5.14]
)
.

For a Dynkin quiver
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
,
{\bm{e}}_{u}

(u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu})
and
an
I
-compatible reading
{\bm{\rho}}
of
{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
, let us set

{\bm{b}}^{\bm{\rho}}_{u}=\widetilde{B}^{{\bm{\rho}}}{\bm{e}}_{u}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}b_{k,u}{\bm{e}}_{k}.

Then we have

(5.10)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\kappa}_{\bm{\rho}}(Z^{{\bm{b}}^{\bm{\rho}}_{u}})=\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}_{i,p-1}^{-1}

where
\widetilde{{\bm{\rho}}}(u)=(i,p)
.

Proof.

We claim that

\widetilde{\kappa}_{\bm{\rho}}(Z^{{\bm{b}}^{\bm{\rho}}_{u}})\cong\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}_{i,p-1}^{-1}.

Note that
b_{u^{+},u}=-1
,
b_{u^{-},u}=1
,
\widetilde{{\bm{\rho}}}(u^{+})=(j,p-2)
and
\widetilde{{\bm{\rho}}}(u^{-})=(j,p+2)
by the assumption on
{\bm{\rho}}
. Then we have

\widetilde{\kappa}_{\bm{\rho}}(Z^{-{\bm{e}}_{u^{+}}+{\bm{e}}_{u^{-}}})\cong\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{-1}\widetilde{X}_{i,p-2}^{-1}.

For
j\sim i
, we see
u^{\pm}(j)
satisfy
u<u^{+}(j)<u^{+}<u^{+}(j)^{+}
,
u^{-}(j)<u<u^{-}(j)^{+}=u^{+}(j)<u^{+}
,
\widetilde{{\bm{\rho}}}(u^{+}(j))=(j,p-3)

and
\widetilde{{\bm{\rho}}}(u^{-}(j))=(j,p-1)
. Since

b_{u^{+}(j),u}=-\mathsf{c}_{ij}
and
b_{u^{-}(j),u}=\mathsf{c}_{ij}
, we have

\widetilde{\kappa}_{\bm{\rho}}(Z^{-\mathsf{c}_{ij}{\bm{e}}_{u^{+}(j)}+\mathsf{c}_{ij}{\bm{e}}_{u^{-}(j)}})\cong\widetilde{X}_{j,p-1}^{-\mathsf{c}_{ij}}.

Hence the claim follows. Since
\widetilde{\kappa}_{{\bm{\rho}}}

is compatible with the bar involutions on
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda^{{\bm{\rho}}})
and
\mathcal{X}_{q,\leqslant\xi}
, we obtain (
5.10
).
∎

Theorem 5.12

(
[
11
, Theorem 5.15]
,
[
26
, Theorem 8.8]
)
.

(i)

For an
I
-compatible reading
{\bm{\rho}}
of
{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
, there exists
a unique
\mathbb{A}
-algebra isomorphism
\kappa_{\bm{\rho}}
which makes the following diagram commutative:

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{{\bm{\rho}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{{\bm{\rho}}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\cdot)_{\leqslant\xi}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}(\Lambda^{{\bm{\rho}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\kappa}_{\bm{\rho}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}_{q,\leqslant\xi}}

(ii)

For
k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
, we have
\kappa_{{\bm{\rho}}}(Z_{k})=F_{q}(\underline{m^{(i)}[p,\xi_{i}]})

where
\widetilde{{\bm{\rho}}}(k)=(i,p)
. Moreover every KR-polynomial
F_{q}(m)
in
\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi}

appears as a cluster variable.

(iii)

\mathsf{F}_{q,\xi}\mathbin{:=}\mathsf{F}_{q}\cap\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi}
,

\mathsf{E}_{q,\xi}\mathbin{:=}\mathsf{E}_{q}\cap\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi}
and

\mathsf{L}_{q,\xi}\mathbin{:=}\mathsf{L}_{q}\cap\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi}
are bases of
\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi}
.

Remark 5.13
.

(a)

The entire ring
\mathfrak{K}_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
has also a quantum cluster algebra structure (see
[
11
, Theorem 6.17]
,
[
26
, Theorem 9.6]
), but we will not use the structure in
this paper.

(b)

The image
(\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi})_{\leqslant\xi}
of
\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi}
under
(\cdot)_{\leqslant\xi}
has a cluster algebra structure with initial cluster variables

\{\underline{m^{(i)}[p,\xi_{i}]}\ |\ i\in I,\ p\leqslant\xi_{i}\}
by (
5.9
) and Theorem
5.12
(
ii
).

Theorem 5.14

(
[
23
,
3
,
26
]
)
.

For each KR-monomial
m^{(i)}[p,s]
and any height function
\xi
on
\triangle
such that
\xi_{i}\geqslant s
,
there exists a quantum cluster algebra algorithm for calculating

(F_{q}(m^{(i)}[p,s]))_{\leqslant\xi}
, which can be obtained from the initial cluster variable
\underline{m^{(i)}[p+2k,\xi_{i}]}
of
(\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi})_{\leqslant\xi}
with
\xi_{i}=s+2k

(k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}_{0})
via a certain sequence of mutations.

Remark 5.15
.

By Theorem
5.6
(
c
),
(F_{q}(m^{(i)}[p,s]))_{\leqslant\xi}=F_{q}(m^{(i)}[p,s])
if
k
in Theorem
5.14
is big enough.

Conjecture 1

(
[
26
]
)
.

(i)

For each KR-monomial
m^{(i)}[p,s]
, the element

F_{q}(m^{(i)}[p,s])
is quantum positive; i.e., each coefficient of
F_{q}(m^{(i)}[p,s])

is contained in
\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}_{0}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
.

(ii)

For each KR-monomial
m^{(i)}[p,s]
, we have
L_{q}(m^{(i)}[p,s])=F_{q}(m^{(i)}[p,s])
.

The conjecture in the above is proved by Nakajima in
[
48
]
for symmetric cases (see also
[
11
]
). By Theorem
5.14
,
Conjecture
1
is closely related to the quantum Laurent positivity conjecture suggested by Berenstein–Fomin–Zelevinsky.

5.5.
Dynkin quivers and their heart subrings

A
Dynkin quiver

Q
is a pair
(\triangle,\xi)
consisting of
(i) a Dynkin diagram
\triangle
of
\mathfrak{g}
and (ii) a height function
\xi
on
\triangle
.

For a Dynkin quiver
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
, we call
i\in I
a
source
(resp.
sink
) of
Q
, if
\xi_{i}>\xi_{j}
(resp.
\xi_{i}<\xi_{j}
) for all
j\in I

with
i\sim j
. For a Dynkin quiver
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
and its source
i
, we denote by
s_{i}Q
the Dynkin quiver
(\triangle,s_{i}\xi)
, where
s_{i}\xi

is the height function defined by

(s_{i}\xi)_{j}\mathbin{:=}\xi_{j}-2\times\delta_{i,j}.

We call the operation from
Q
to
s_{i}Q
the reflection of
Q
at the source
i

of
Q
.
For any Dynkin quivers
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
with the same Dynkin diagram
\triangle
,
there exists a sequence of reflections, including
s_{i}^{-1}
, from
Q
to
Q^{\prime}
.

For a sequence of indices
\textbf{{i}}=(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{l})\in I^{l}

(l\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}\sqcup\{\infty\})
and a Dynkin quiver
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
, we call
i

Q
-adapted
if

i_{k}
is a source of
s_{i_{k-1}}s_{i_{k-2}}\cdots s_{i_{1}}Q
for all

1\leqslant k\leqslant l
.

The followings are known (see
[
22
,
36
,
39
]
):

(A)

Any
I
-compatible reading of
{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
is
Q
-adapted.

(B)

For each Dynkin quiver
Q
, there exists a unique Coxeter element

\tau_{Q}\in\mathsf{W}
, all
of whose reduced sequences are adapted to
Q
. Conversely, for any Coxeter element
\tau
, there exists a unique Dynkin quiver
Q
such that
\tau=\tau_{Q}
.

(C)

For each Dynkin quiver
Q
, there exists a reduced sequence
i
of
w_{\circ}
adapted to
Q
and the set of all reduced sequence of
w_{\circ}
adapted to
Q
forms a single commutation class of
w_{\circ}
, denoted by
[Q]
.

(D)

For a reduced sequence
\textbf{{i}}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell})
of
w_{\circ}
adapted to
Q
, define

{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}=(\imath_{1},\imath_{2},\ldots)\in I^{(\infty)}
by

(5.11)

\displaystyle\imath_{k}=i_{k}\quad\text{ for }1\leqslant k\leqslant\ell\quad\text{ and }\quad\imath_{k}=\imath^{*}_{k-\ell}\quad\text{ for all }k>\ell.

Then
{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}
is an
I
-compatible reading of
{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
and we have a compatible reading
{\widetilde{\textbf{{i}}}}
of
{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
corresponding to
{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}
.
Furthermore, we have
{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}\overset{c}{\sim}{\bm{\rho}}
for any
I
-compatible reading
{\bm{\rho}}
of
{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
.

(E)

For each Dynkin quiver
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
, there exists a bijection
\phi_{Q}:{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}\to\Phi^{+}\times\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
defined recursively as follows: Set
\upgamma_{i}^{Q}\mathbin{:=}(1-\tau_{Q})\varpi_{i}
and

(i)

\phi_{Q}(i,\xi_{i})\mathbin{:=}(\upgamma_{i}^{Q},0)
for each
i\in I
,

(ii)

if
\phi_{i}(i,p)=({\mspace{1mu}\alpha},k)
, we have

(5.12)

\displaystyle\phi_{Q}(i,p\pm 2)\mathbin{:=}\begin{cases}(\tau_{Q}^{\mp}{\mspace{1mu}\alpha},k)\text{ if }\tau_{Q}^{\mp}\in\Phi^{+},\\
(-\tau_{Q}^{\mp}{\mspace{1mu}\alpha},k\pm 1)\text{ if }\tau_{Q}^{\mp}\in\Phi^{-}.\end{cases}

For a Dynkin quiver
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
, we denote by
\mathfrak{D}^{\pm 1}Q
the Dynkin quiver
(\triangle,\mathfrak{D}^{\pm 1}\xi)
, where
\mathfrak{D}^{\pm 1}\xi
is the height function defined as follows:

(\mathfrak{D}^{\pm 1}\xi)_{j}\mathbin{:=}\xi_{j^{*}}\pm\mathsf{h}\quad\text{ for }j\in I.

Proposition 5.16

(
[
22
,
12
,
39
]
)
.

Let
Q
be a Dynkin quiver and set

\Gamma_{0}^{Q}\mathbin{:=}\phi_{Q}^{-1}(\Phi^{+}\times\{0\})
. Then
\Gamma_{0}

is a subset of
{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
characterized by

\Gamma^{Q}_{0}=\{(i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}\ |\ \xi_{i^{*}}-\mathsf{h}<p\leqslant\xi_{i}\}.

Furthermore, for any
(j,s)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
, there exists unique
k\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}

and
(i,p)\in\Gamma^{Q}_{0}
such that

\mathfrak{D}^{k}(X_{i,p})=X_{j,s}.

We define
\mathcal{X}_{q,Q}

the quantum subtorus of
\mathcal{X}_{q}
generated by
\{\widetilde{X}_{i,p}\ |\ (i,p)\in\Gamma^{Q}_{0}\}
, and set

\mathcal{M}^{Q}\mathbin{:=}\mathcal{M}\cap\mathcal{X}_{q,Q}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{M}^{Q}_{+}\mathbin{:=}\mathcal{M}_{+}\cap\mathcal{X}_{q,Q}.

We denote by

\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}
the subring of
\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi}
which is generated by
\{F_{q}(X_{i,p})\ |\ (i,p)\in\Gamma^{Q}_{0}\}
.
We call it
the
heart
subring associated with the Dynkin quiver
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
.

Theorem 5.17

(
[
22
,
24
,
10
,
11
,
26
,
25
]
)
.

Let
i
be a reduced sequence of
w_{\circ}
adapted to
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
.

(i)

We have
\mathbb{A}
-algebra isomorphisms
\widetilde{\kappa}_{\textbf{{i}}}:\mathcal{T}(\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}})\to\mathcal{X}_{q,Q}
and

\kappa_{\textbf{{i}}}:\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}}\to\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}
, and an
\mathbb{A}
-algebra monomorphism

(\cdot)_{\leqslant\xi}|_{\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}}
which make the following diagram commutative:

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{\textbf{{i}}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\cdot)_{\leqslant\xi}|_{\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\cdot)_{\leqslant\xi}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}(\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\kappa}_{\textbf{{i}}}\hskip 16.38895pt}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}_{q,Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}({{}^{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}\hskip-0.86108pt\Lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\kappa}_{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}_{q,\leqslant\xi}}

(ii)

The set
\mathsf{F}_{q,Q}\mathbin{:=}\mathsf{F}_{q}\cap\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}
,

\mathsf{E}_{q,Q}\mathbin{:=}\mathsf{E}_{q}\cap\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}
and

\mathsf{L}_{q,Q}\mathbin{:=}\mathsf{L}_{q}\cap\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}
are bases of
\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}
.

The following theorem is proved in
[
25
, Corollary 5.4]
which gives a partial answer for Conjecture
1
(
ii
).

Theorem 5.18

(
[
25
, Corollary 5.4]
)
.

For a KR-monomial
m
contained in
\mathcal{M}_{+}^{Q}
for some Dynkin quiver
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
, we have

L_{q}(m)=F_{q}(m).

Recall that, for a reduced sequence
i
of
w_{\circ}
adapted to a Dynkin quiver
Q
, we have an
\mathbb{A}
-algebra isomorphism

\varphi_{\textbf{{i}}}:\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}}\mathop{\xrightarrow[\raisebox{0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.90417pt]{$\scriptstyle{}$}]{{\raisebox{-1.80835pt}[0.0pt][-1.80835pt]{$\mspace{2mu}\sim\mspace{2mu}$}}}}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathfrak{n}).

Hence we have an
\mathbb{A}
-algebra isomorphism

(5.13)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\textbf{{i}}}\mathbin{:=}\kappa_{\textbf{{i}}}\circ\varphi_{\textbf{{i}}}^{-1}:\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathfrak{n})\mathop{\xrightarrow[\raisebox{0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.90417pt]{$\scriptstyle{}$}]{{\raisebox{-1.80835pt}[0.0pt][-1.80835pt]{$\mspace{2mu}\sim\mspace{2mu}$}}}}\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}.

Moreover, for another reduced sequence
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}
of
w_{\circ}
adapted to
Q
,
we have
\Psi_{\textbf{{i}}}=\Psi_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}
(see
[
10
,
25
]
for more details about the independence of the choice of
\textbf{{i}}\in[Q]
for the isomorphism
\Psi_{\textbf{{i}}}
).
Thus the notation
\Psi_{Q}
make sense.

Theorem 5.19

(
[
22
]
,
[
10
, §10.3]
,
[
25
, §7]
)
.

(a)

For each Dynkin quiver
Q
, the isomorphism
\Psi_{Q}
sends
\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\rm up}

to
\mathsf{L}_{q,Q}
.

(b)

For a reduced sequence
i
of
w_{\circ}
adapted to
Q
and a KR-monomial
m^{(i)}[p,s]
in
\mathcal{M}^{Q}_{+}
, there exists
a pair of integers
(t,u)
such that
0\leqslant t<u\leqslant\ell
,

{\rm(i)}\ \ i_{t}=i_{u}\ \ \text{ if }\ t>0\quad\text{ and }\quad{\rm(ii)}\ \ \Psi_{Q}(\widetilde{D}(w^{\textbf{{i}}}_{u}\varpi_{i_{u}},w^{\textbf{{i}}}_{t}\varpi_{i_{u}}))=F_{q}(m^{(i)}[p,s]).

Here we understand
w^{\textbf{{i}}}_{0}={\rm id}\in\mathsf{W}
.

(c)

Let
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
and
Q^{\prime}=(\triangle,\xi^{\prime})
be Dynkin quivers defined on the same
\triangle
.
Then we have a unique
\mathbb{A}
-algebra automorphism

(5.14)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}:\mathfrak{K}_{q}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{K}_{q}

satisfying the following properties:

(i)

The automorphisms
\Psi_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}
restricted to
\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q^{\prime}}
and
\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi}
respectively induce
\mathbb{A}
-algebra isomorphisms

(5.15)

\displaystyle\Psi_{Q,Q^{\prime}}:\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q^{\prime}}\mathop{\xrightarrow[\raisebox{0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.90417pt]{$\scriptstyle{}$}]{{\raisebox{-1.80835pt}[0.0pt][-1.80835pt]{$\mspace{2mu}\sim\mspace{2mu}$}}}}\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}\quad\text{ and }\quad\Psi_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}:\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi}\mathop{\xrightarrow[\raisebox{0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.90417pt]{$\scriptstyle{}$}]{{\raisebox{-1.80835pt}[0.0pt][-1.80835pt]{$\mspace{2mu}\sim\mspace{2mu}$}}}}{{}^{\xi^{\prime}}}\hskip-0.86108pt\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}

such that
\Psi_{Q,Q^{\prime}}=\Psi_{Q}\circ\Psi_{Q^{\prime}}^{-1}
.

(ii)

We have
\Psi_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}\circ\mathfrak{D}^{\pm 1}_{q}=\mathfrak{D}^{\pm 1}_{q}\circ\Psi_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}
.

(iii)

\Psi_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}
sends
\mathsf{L}_{q}
to
\mathsf{L}_{q}
bijectively.

Theorem 5.20

(
[
34
,
25
]
)
.

Let
Q
be a Dynkin quiver with a source
i\in I
and write
\textbf{{T}}_{i}
for the automorphism
\Phi_{s_{i}\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}}
of
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
. Then
the collection of automorphism
\{\textbf{{T}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
satisfies the braid relations of
\mathfrak{g}
.

For Dynkin quivers
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
and
Q^{\prime}=(\triangle,\xi^{\prime})
on the same
\triangle
, let
\textbf{{i}}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell})

and
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}=(i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{\ell})
be reduced sequences of
w_{\circ}
adapted to
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
, respectively. Then we have a sequence

{\bm{\tau}}=(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{r})\text{ in }\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{\ell-1}\}\sqcup\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{\ell-2}\}\sqcup\{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{\ell-3}\}\cup\{\zeta_{1}\}

such that

\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}=\tau_{1}\cdots\tau_{r}\textbf{{i}}.

Recall we have
{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}=(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots),{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}=(i^{\prime}_{1},i^{\prime}_{2},\ldots)\in I^{(\infty)}
corresponding to
i
and
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}
respectively,
which are constructed in (
5.11
). We extend the isomorphism

{\bm{\tau}}^{*}:\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}}
to the one
from
\mathcal{A}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}}
to
\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}
as follows: For each
n\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
, consider
the
\mathbb{A}
-algebra isomorphism

\widehat{{\bm{\tau}}}^{(n)*}:=\widehat{\tau}_{r}^{(n)*}\circ\cdots\circ\widehat{\tau}_{1}^{(n)*}:\mathcal{A}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}}^{n\ell}\to\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{n\ell},

where
\ell=\ell(w_{\circ})
and

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}^{(n)*}_{k}\mathbin{:=}\gamma_{k}^{*}\gamma_{k+\ell}^{*}\cdots\gamma_{k+(n-1)\ell}^{*},

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mspace{1mu}\beta}}^{(n)*}_{k}\mathbin{:=}{\mspace{1mu}\beta}_{k}^{*}{\mspace{1mu}\beta}_{k+\ell}^{*}\cdots{\mspace{1mu}\beta}_{k+(n-1)\ell}^{*},

\displaystyle\widehat{\eta}^{(n)*}_{k}\mathbin{:=}\eta_{k}^{*}\eta_{k+\ell}^{*}\cdots\eta_{k+(n-1)\ell}^{*},

\displaystyle\widehat{\zeta}^{(n)*}_{1}\mathbin{:=}\zeta_{1}^{*}\zeta_{1+\ell}^{*}\cdots\zeta_{1+(n-1)\ell}^{*}.

Recalling
\tau_{k}\circ\tau_{l}=\tau_{l}\circ\tau_{k}
when
|k-l|\geqslant\ell
, we define

\widehat{{\bm{\tau}}}^{*}\mathbin{:=}\lim_{n\to\infty}\widehat{{\bm{\tau}}}^{(n)*}:\mathcal{A}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{A}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}.

The following theorem tells us that the isomorphism
\widehat{{\bm{\tau}}}^{*}
is compatible with the isomorphisms
\Psi_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}
,
\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}}
and

\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}
. The argument of a proof can be almost the same as the one in
[
11
, Theorem 6.3]
,
which investigates simply-laced types only.
For the reader’s convenience, we give a proof of the theorem.

Theorem 5.21
.

We have the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{{\bm{\tau}}}^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi}}

Proof.

Setting
\Uppsi\mathbin{:=}\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}\circ\widehat{{\bm{\tau}}}^{*}\circ\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}^{-1}
, it is enough to prove that

\Uppsi=\Psi_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}.

By Theorem
5.19
, we have to show the following:

(1)

\Uppsi|_{\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}}=\Psi_{Q,Q^{\prime}}
,

(2)

\Uppsi\circ\mathfrak{D}_{q}^{-1}=\mathfrak{D}_{q}^{-1}\circ\Uppsi
.

Noting that
\mathcal{A}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}}^{\ell}\simeq\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}
,
\mathcal{A}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}^{\ell}\simeq\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}}
,

\widehat{{\bm{\tau}}}^{*}|_{\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}}={\bm{\tau}}^{*}
,
\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}}|_{\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}}=\kappa_{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}
,

\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}|_{\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}}}=\kappa_{\textbf{{i}}}

and
\Psi_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}|_{\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q^{\prime}}}=\Psi_{Q,Q^{\prime}}
,
we have

Here the commutativity for
A
-triangles follows from (
5.13
),
the commutativity for
B
-triangle from Lemma
4.6
and
the commutativity for
C
-triangle from the definition in (
5.15
).
Thus we have shown (
1
).

Now let us consider (
2
). Setting
{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{*}\mathbin{:=}(i^{*}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
, we have
{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}=\partial_{+}^{\ell}{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{*}
,

\widetilde{B}^{\textbf{{i}}}=\widetilde{B}^{\textbf{{i}}^{*}}
and
\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}}=\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}^{*}}
. Thus we have an isomorphism
\nu:\mathcal{A}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{*}}\simeq\mathcal{A}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}
sending
Z_{k}
to itself.
By Proposition
3.24
and Theorem
5.12
, the cluster variables

\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}^{-1}\mathfrak{D}_{q}^{-1}F_{q}(X_{i,p})\quad\text{ and }\quad\nu(\partial_{+}^{*})^{\ell}\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}^{-1}F_{q}(X_{i,p})

have the same degrees for all
(i,p)\in{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
, and hence they are the same by Theorem
2.7
. Since the set

\{F_{q}(X_{i,p})\}_{(i,p)\in{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.60275pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}}

generates
\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi}
, we conclude that

(5.26)

\displaystyle\nu\circ(\partial_{+}^{*})^{\ell}\circ\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}^{-1}=\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}^{-1}\circ\mathfrak{D}_{q}^{-1}.

Note that the same assertion for
{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}
holds by the same argument. Furthermore, for a cluster monomial
x\in\mathcal{A}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}}

whose degree belongs to
C_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}}
, one can see that the degrees of the cluster variables

(5.27)

\displaystyle\widehat{{\bm{\tau}}}^{*}\nu(\partial_{+}^{*})^{\ell}x\quad\text{ and }\quad\nu(\partial_{+}^{*})^{\ell}\widehat{{\bm{\tau}}}^{*}x\quad\text{ are the same}

by Proposition
3.24
and by the result in Section
3
that
\widehat{{\bm{\tau}}}^{*}
sends
C_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}}

to
C_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}
.
Now we have

\displaystyle\Uppsi\mathfrak{D}_{q}^{-1}F_{q}(X_{i,p})

\displaystyle=\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}\widehat{{\bm{\tau}}}^{*}\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}}^{-1}\mathfrak{D}_{q}^{-1}F_{q}(X_{i,p})

\displaystyle=\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}\widehat{{\bm{\tau}}}^{*}\nu(\partial_{+}^{*})^{\ell}\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}}^{-1}F_{q}(X_{i,p})

by (
5.26
) for
{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}\nu(\partial_{+}^{*})^{\ell}\widehat{{\bm{\tau}}}^{*}\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}}^{-1}F_{q}(X_{i,p})

by (
5.27
)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{D}_{q}^{-1}\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}\widehat{{\bm{\tau}}}^{*}\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}}^{-1}F_{q}(X_{i,p})

by (
5.26
) for
{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{D}_{q}^{-1}\Uppsi F_{q}(X_{i,p})

for any
(i,p)\in{{}^{\xi^{\prime}}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
. Since the set

\{F_{q}(X_{i,p})\}_{(i,p)\in{{}^{\xi^{\prime}}}\hskip-0.60275pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}}
generates
\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi^{\prime}}
, the assertion (
2
) follows.
∎

Remark 5.22
.

In the above theorem, we have applied a special sequence
(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots)\in I^{(\infty)}
such that
(i_{k},\ldots,i_{k+\ell-1})
is
a reduced sequence of
w_{\circ}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
.

Corollary 5.23
.

Let
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
be Dynkin quivers on the same Dynkin diagram
\triangle
.
For reduced sequences
i
and
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}
adapted to
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
respectively, the
\mathbb{A}
-algebra isomorphism
\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}\circ\kappa_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}}
induces a bijection between the set of quantum cluster monomials of
\mathcal{A}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}}
and that of
\mathcal{A}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}
.

5.6.
Substitution formulas

One of the celebrated results of Berenstein–Fomin–Zelevinsky is to show that quantum cluster algebra
is still contained in
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}][Z^{\pm 1}_{k}]_{k\in\mathsf{K}}
even though mutations involves non-trivial fractions.
In this subsection, we shall show that the automorphism
\Psi_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}
can be interpreted as an restriction of birational
auto-transformation of
\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}_{q})
, which is referred to as
substitution formula
in
[
11
, Section 7]
. Since
\Psi_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}

can be understood as a composition of
\{\textbf{{T}}^{\pm 1}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, it gives
an interesting relation among elements in
\mathsf{L}_{q}
. The result in this section for simply-laced type is given in
[
11
, Section 7]
and
we apply the same framework and arguments of proofs in this paper for non simply-laced type, based on the results that have been obtained for
\mathfrak{K}_{q}
,

4
-moves and
6
-moves.
Thus we just give statements, several notations and maps, so that readers may recover the substitution formula on
\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}_{q})
and
\mathfrak{K}_{q}

by themselves.

Throughout this subsection, we (i) fix Dynkin quivers
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
and
Q^{\prime}=(\triangle,\xi^{\prime})
defined on the same Dynkin diagram
\triangle

of non simply-laced type, (ii) denote by

\textbf{{i}}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell})
and
\textbf{{j}}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{\ell})
reduced sequences of
w_{\circ}
adapted to
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
respectively. To distinguish generators of quantum tori
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda^{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}})
and
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda^{\widehat{\textbf{{j}}}})
, we use
Z_{k}
for initial cluster variables
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda^{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}})
while
Z^{\prime}_{k}
for ones of
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda^{\widehat{\textbf{{j}}}})
.

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
,
we set
\xi[k]\mathbin{:=}\mathfrak{D}^{k}\xi
and
Q[k]\mathbin{:=}\mathfrak{D}^{k}Q
for notational simplicity. We recall the following:

(a)

We have injective homomorphisms

\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi[-1]}\hookrightarrow(\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi})_{\leqslant\xi}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{q,\leqslant\xi}

induced from
(\cdot)_{\leqslant\xi}
, whose composition is an identity indeed, by Theorem
5.6
and the fact that
\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi}
is generated by
\{F_{q}(X_{i,p})\ |\ (i,p)\in{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}\}
.

(b)

We have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{(\cdot)_{\leqslant\xi}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}_{q,\leqslant\xi}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi[-1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathfrak{K}_{q,\xi})_{\leqslant\xi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Then, for
I
-compatible readings
{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}
(resp.
{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime}
) of
{{}^{\xi}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
(resp.
{{}^{\xi^{\prime}}}\hskip-0.86108pt{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
),
we have unique morphisms
\widetilde{\kappa}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}},\mathbb{F}}
,
\widetilde{\kappa}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}^{\prime},\mathbb{F}}
,
\widehat{{\bm{\tau}}}^{*}_{\mathbb{F}}
and
\widetilde{\Psi}_{\xi,\xi}

which make the diagram below commutative:

Let us set

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Sigma}\mathbin{:=}\widetilde{\kappa}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}},\mathbb{F}}^{-1}\circ\mathfrak{D}^{-1}_{q}\circ\widetilde{\kappa}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}},\mathbb{F}}:\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{T}(\Lambda^{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}))\to\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{T}(\Lambda^{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}})),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Sigma}^{\prime}\mathbin{:=}\widetilde{\kappa}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{j}}}},\mathbb{F}}^{-1}\circ\mathfrak{D}^{-1}_{q}\circ\widetilde{\kappa}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{j}}}},\mathbb{F}}:\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{T}(\Lambda^{{\widehat{\textbf{{j}}}}}))\to\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{T}(\Lambda^{{\widehat{\textbf{{j}}}}})),

which are automorphisms of skew field of fractions. We also define
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
-module homomorphisms
\Upsigma,\widetilde{\Upsigma}:\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}\to\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}

given by

\Upsigma({\bm{e}}_{u})={\bm{e}}_{u+\ell}\qquad\widetilde{\Upsigma}({\bm{e}}_{u})={\bm{e}}_{u+\ell}-{\bm{e}}_{(\ell+1)_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}^{-}(i^{*}_{u})}\quad\text{ for all }u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}.

Note that

(5.36)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Upsigma}({\bm{a}})=\Upsigma({\bm{a}})-\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{p}_{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}({\bm{a}};i){\bm{e}}_{(\ell+1)_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}^{-}(i^{*})}\quad\text{ for ${\bm{a}}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\mathbb{N}}\mspace{1mu}$}.

The proof of the following lemma utilizes Proposition
5.11
.

Lemma 5.24
.

For
{\bm{a}}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
, we have

\widetilde{\Sigma}(Z^{{\bm{a}}})=Z^{\widetilde{\Upsigma}({\bm{a}})}\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{\Sigma}^{\prime}(Z^{\prime{\bm{a}}})=Z^{\prime\widetilde{\Upsigma}({\bm{a}})}

In particular,

(5.37)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Upsigma}({\bm{b}}^{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}_{u})={\bm{b}}^{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}_{u+\ell}\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{\Upsigma}({\bm{b}}^{{\widehat{\textbf{{j}}}}}_{u})={\bm{b}}^{{\widehat{\textbf{{j}}}}}_{u+\ell}.

For
u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
and an initial cluster variable
Z^{\prime}_{u}
of
\mathcal{A}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{j}}}}}
, we set
\mathcal{Z}_{u}\mathbin{:=}\widehat{{\bm{\tau}}}^{*}(Z^{\prime}_{u})
, which is a cluster variable of

\mathcal{A}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}
. Then there exists
{\bm{g}}_{u}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
and
c_{u,{\bm{n}}}
satisfying

\mathcal{Z}_{u}=Z^{{\bm{g}}_{u}}\left(1+\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}_{0}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}\setminus\{0\}}c_{u,\mathbf{n}}Z^{\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}n_{k}{\bm{b}}^{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}_{k}}\right)

by Theorem
2.7
.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}_{0}
, we set

\Gamma^{Q[-k]}_{0,\mathrm{fr}}\mathbin{:=}\{(i,\xi[-k-1]_{i}+2)\ |\ i\in I\},\quad\Gamma^{Q[-k]}_{0,\mathrm{ex}}=\Gamma^{Q[-k]}_{0}\setminus\Gamma^{Q[-k]}_{0,\mathrm{fr}}.

Note that, for each
u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
, there exists a unique
k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}_{0}
such that
\mathcal{Z}_{u}
can be obtained from the initial cluster
\{Z_{u}\ |\ u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}\}
by applying the mutations
only at the vertices labelled by
\Gamma^{Q[-k]}_{0,\mathrm{ex}}
. By
\ell
-periodicity of
{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}
,
{\widehat{\textbf{{j}}}}
and
\widehat{{\bm{\tau}}}^{*}
, for
u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
, we have

(5.38)

\displaystyle{\bm{g}}_{u+\ell}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}=\Upsigma({\bm{g}}_{u})&\text{ if }u>\ell\text{ or }u=(\ell+1)_{{\widehat{\textbf{{j}}}}}^{-}(i)\text{ for }i\in I,\\
\in\Upsigma({\bm{g}}_{u})+\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}{\bm{e}}_{(\ell+1)^{-}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}(i)}&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

and

(5.39)

\displaystyle c_{u+\ell,{\bm{n}}}=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}c_{u,\Upsigma^{-1}({\bm{n}})}&\text{ if }{\bm{n}}\in\Upsigma(\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}_{0}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}),\\
0&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Remark 5.25
.

Any braid move between
{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}
and
{\widehat{\textbf{{j}}}}
does not occur at
(\ell+1)_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}^{-}(i)+k\ell
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}_{0}
and
i\in I
.
Hence
\mathcal{Z}_{(\ell+1)_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}^{-}(i)+k\ell}=Z_{(\ell+1)_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}^{-}(i)+k\ell}=Z^{\prime}_{(\ell+1)_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}^{-}(i)+k\ell}
in
\mathcal{A}_{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}
and they

q
-commute with all
Z_{a}

(a\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu})
.

When
\mathfrak{g}
is of non-simply-laced type,
we need to employ Lemma
3.12
, Lemma
3.20

and Lemma
3.21
to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 5.26
.

For
u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}
, we have

(5.40)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Sigma}(\mathcal{Z}_{u})

\displaystyle=Z^{\widetilde{\Upsigma}({\bm{g}}_{u})}\left(1+\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}_{0}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}\setminus\{0\}}c_{u+\ell,\mathbf{n}}Z^{\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}n_{k}{\bm{b}}^{{\widehat{\textbf{{i}}}}}_{k}}\right)\simeq Z^{\widetilde{\Upsigma}({\bm{g}}_{u})-{\bm{g}}_{u+\ell}}\mathcal{Z}_{u+\ell}.

Remark 5.27
.

The following proposition is a non-symmetric analogue of
[
11
, Lemma 7.4 (1)]
, and it is
not at all
a consequence of Theorem
5.21
as emphasized in the introduction of
[
11
, Section 7]
.

Proposition 5.28
.

We have

\mathfrak{D}_{q}^{-1}|_{\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}_{q,\leqslant\xi})}\circ\widetilde{\Psi}_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}=\widetilde{\Psi}_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}\circ\mathfrak{D}_{q}^{-1}|_{\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}_{q,\leqslant\xi^{\prime}})}.

Corollary 5.29
.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}_{0}
and
(i,p)\in\Gamma^{Q^{\prime}[-k]}_{0}
, we have

\widetilde{\Psi}_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}(\widetilde{X}_{i,p})\in\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}_{q,Q[-k]}).

Proof.

Since
\widetilde{\Psi}_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}(\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}_{q,Q^{\prime}}))\subset\mathcal{X}_{q,Q}
, it follows from Proposition
5.28
.
∎

Theorem 5.30
.

There exists an automorphism of the skew field
\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}_{q})

\widetilde{\Psi}_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}:\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}_{q})\mathop{\xrightarrow[\raisebox{0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.90417pt]{$\scriptstyle{}$}]{{\raisebox{-1.80835pt}[0.0pt][-1.80835pt]{$\mspace{2mu}\sim\mspace{2mu}$}}}}\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}_{q})

satisfying the following properties:

(a)

\mathfrak{D}_{q}\circ\widetilde{\Psi}_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}=\widetilde{\Psi}_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}\circ\mathfrak{D}_{q}
.

(b)

\widetilde{\Psi}_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}(\widetilde{X}_{i,p})\in\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}_{q,Q[k]})
for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
and
(i,p)\in\Gamma^{Q^{\prime}[k]}_{0}
.

(c)

We have the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}_{q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\Psi}_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}_{q})}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{K}_{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{K}_{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Proof.

We first define
\widetilde{\Psi}_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}
from
\widetilde{\Psi}_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}
by using
\mathfrak{D}_{q}
as follows: For each
y\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X}_{q})
, there exists

k\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}_{0}
such that
\mathfrak{D}_{q}^{-k}(y)\in\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}_{q,\leqslant\xi})
. Then we define

\widetilde{\Psi}_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}(y)=\mathfrak{D}_{q}^{k}(\widetilde{\Psi}_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{D}_{q}^{-k}(y))).

Now, as in
[
11
, Theorem 7.1]
, one can prove that it is a well-defined automorphism of
\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}_{q})
satisfying the properties in the statement.
∎

In Appendix
A
, we provide examples of substitution formulas.

6.
Categorification via quiver Hecke algebras and Laurent family

In this section, we first review the results on the categorification theory via the quiver Hecke algebra
R
related to our purpose. Then we recall the definitions and the results about the Laurent family of simple
R
-modules,
which were introduced and investigated in
[
37
]
very recently.

6.1.
Quiver Hecke algebra

Let
\mathbf{k}

be a base field. For
i,j\in I
, we choose polynomials
\mathcal{Q}_{i,j}(u,v)\in\mathbf{k}[u,v]
such that

(a)

\mathcal{Q}_{i,j}(u,v)=\mathcal{Q}_{j,i}(v,u)
,

(b)

\mathcal{Q}_{i,j}(u,v)=\displaystyle\delta(i\neq j)\hskip-4.30554pt\sum_{2\mathsf{d}_{i}p+2\mathsf{d}_{j}q=-2({\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{i},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{j})}\hskip-4.30554ptt_{i,j;p,q}u^{p}v^{q}
where
t_{i,j;-a_{i,j},0}\in\mathbf{k}^{\times}
.

For
{\mspace{1mu}\beta}\in\mathsf{Q}^{+}
with
|{\mspace{1mu}\beta}|=n
, we set

I^{\mspace{1mu}\beta}\mathbin{:=}\{\nu=(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{n})\in I^{n}\ |\ \sum_{k=1}^{n}{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{\nu_{k}}={\mspace{1mu}\beta}\}.

For
\mathfrak{g}
and
{\mspace{1mu}\beta}\in\mathsf{Q}^{+}
with
|{\mspace{1mu}\beta}|=r
, the quiver Hecke algebra
R({\mspace{1mu}\beta})

associated with
(\mathcal{Q}_{i,j})_{i,j\in I}
is the
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
-graded algebra
over
\mathbf{k}
generated by

x_{k}\ \ (1\leqslant k\leqslant r),\quad e(\nu)\ \ (\nu\in I^{{\mspace{1mu}\beta}}),\quad\tau_{s}\ (1\leqslant s<r),

subject to certain defining relations (see
[
29
]
for the relations). Note that
the
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
-grading of
R({\mspace{1mu}\beta})
is determined by the degrees of following elements:

\deg(e(\nu))=0,\ \ \deg(x_{k}e(\nu))=2\mathsf{d}_{\nu_{k}}\ (1\leqslant k\leqslant n),\ \text{ and }\ \deg(\tau_{m}e(\nu))=-({\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{\nu_{m}},{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{\nu_{m+1}})\ (1\leqslant m<n).

We denote by
R({\mspace{1mu}\beta})\text{-}\mathrm{gmod}
the category of finite-dimensional graded
R({\mspace{1mu}\beta})
-modules with homogeneous homomorphisms. For
M\in R({\mspace{1mu}\beta})\text{-}\mathrm{gmod}
, we set

{\rm wt}(M)=-{\mspace{1mu}\beta}\in\mathsf{Q}^{-}
. Denote by
q
the degree shift functor
acting on
R({\mspace{1mu}\beta})\text{-}\mathrm{gmod}
as follows:

q(M)_{n}=M_{n-1}\qquad\text{ for }M=\mathop{\mbox{\normalsize$\bigoplus$}}\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}}M_{k}\in R({\mspace{1mu}\beta})\text{-}\mathrm{gmod}.

For an
R({\mspace{1mu}\beta})
-module
M
and an
R(\gamma)
-module
N
, we obtain an
R({\mspace{1mu}\beta}+\gamma)
-module by

M\mathop{\mathbin{\mbox{\large$\circ$}}}N\mathbin{:=}R({\mspace{1mu}\beta}+\gamma)e({\mspace{1mu}\beta},\gamma)\otimes_{R({\mspace{1mu}\beta})\otimes R(\gamma)}(M\mathop{\mathbin{\mbox{\large$\circ$}}}N),

where
e({\mspace{1mu}\beta},\gamma)\mathbin{:=}\sum_{\nu\in I^{\mspace{1mu}\beta},\nu^{\prime}\in I^{\gamma}}e(\nu*\nu^{\prime})\in R({\mspace{1mu}\beta}+\gamma)
. Here
\nu*\mu
denotes the concatenation of
\nu
and
\mu
, and

\mathop{\mathbin{\mbox{\large$\circ$}}}
is called the
convolution product
. We say that two simple
R
-modules
M
and
N

strongly commute
if
M\mathop{\mathbin{\mbox{\large$\circ$}}}N
is simple. If a simple module
M
strongly commutes with itself, then
M
is called
real
.

Set
R\text{-}\mathrm{gmod}\mathbin{:=}\oplus_{{\mspace{1mu}\beta}\in\mathsf{Q}^{+}}R({\mspace{1mu}\beta})\text{-}\mathrm{gmod}
. Then
R\text{-}\mathrm{gmod}

has the monoidal category structure with the unit object
\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{k}\in R(0)\text{-}\mathrm{gmod}
and the tensor product
\mathop{\mathbin{\mbox{\large$\circ$}}}
. Thus the Grothendieck group
K(R\text{-}\mathrm{gmod})

has the
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1}]
-algebra structure derived from
\mathop{\mathbin{\mbox{\large$\circ$}}}
and the degree shift functors
q^{\pm 1}
. We set

\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{A}}(R\text{-}\mathrm{gmod})\mathbin{:=}\mathbb{A}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1}]}K(R\text{-}\mathrm{gmod}).

Theorem 6.1

(
[
41
,
42
,
52
]
)
.

There exists an
\mathbb{A}
-algebra isomorphism

\Upomega:\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{A}}(R\text{-}\mathrm{gmod})\mathop{\xrightarrow[\raisebox{0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.90417pt]{$\scriptstyle{}$}]{{\raisebox{-1.80835pt}[0.0pt][-1.80835pt]{$\mspace{2mu}\sim\mspace{2mu}$}}}}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathfrak{n}).

Proposition 6.2

(
[
32
]
)
.

For
\lambda\in\mathsf{P}^{+}
and
\mu,\zeta\in\mathsf{W}\lambda
with
\mu\preccurlyeq\zeta
, there
exists a self-dual real simple
R(\zeta-\mu)
-module
L(\mu,\zeta)
such that

\Upomega([L(\mu,\zeta)])=D(\mu,\zeta).

Here
[L(\mu,\zeta)]
denotes the isomorphism class of
L(\mu,\zeta)
.

We call
L(\mu,\zeta)
the
determinantal module
associated with
D(\mu,\zeta)
.

6.2.
R
-matrices and their
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
-invariants

For
{\mspace{1mu}\beta}\in\mathsf{Q}^{+}
and
i\in I
, let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{i,{\mspace{1mu}\beta}}=\sum_{\nu\,\in I^{\mspace{1mu}\beta}}\big(\prod_{a\in[1,|{\mspace{1mu}\beta}|];\ \nu_{a}=i}x_{a}\big)e(\nu)\in\mathfrak{X}(R({\mspace{1mu}\beta})),

where
\mathfrak{X}(R({\mspace{1mu}\beta}))
denotes the center of
R({\mspace{1mu}\beta})
.

Definition 6.3

(
[
40
, Definition 2.2]
)
.

For an
R({\mspace{1mu}\beta})
-module
L
, we say that
L

admits an affinization
if there exists an
R({\mspace{1mu}\beta})
-module
\widehat{L}

satisfying the condition: there exists an endomorphism
z_{\widehat{L}}
of degree
t\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{>0}
such that
\widehat{L}/z_{\widehat{L}}\widehat{L}\simeq L
and

(i)

\widehat{L}
is a finitely generated free modules over the polynomial ring
\mathbf{k}[z_{\widehat{L}}]
,

(ii)

\mathfrak{p}_{i,{\mspace{1mu}\beta}}\widehat{L}\neq 0
for all
i\in I
.

We say that a simple
R({\mspace{1mu}\beta})
-module
L
is
affreal
if
L
is real and admits an affinization.

It is known that any
L\in R({\mspace{1mu}\beta})\text{-}\mathrm{gmod}
admits an affinization if
R({\mspace{1mu}\beta})
is symmetric. However, when
R({\mspace{1mu}\beta})
is not symmetric, it is widely open whether an
R({\mspace{1mu}\beta})
-module
L
admits an affinization or not.

Theorem 6.4

(
[
35
, Theorem 3.26]
)
.

For
\varpi\in\mathsf{P}^{+}
and
\mu,\zeta\in\mathsf{W}\varpi
such that
\mu\preccurlyeq\zeta
, the determinantal module
L(\mu,\zeta)
is affreal.

Let
i
be a reduced sequence
of
w_{\circ}
. For
{\mspace{1mu}\beta}\in\Phi^{+}
such that
{\mspace{1mu}\beta}={\mspace{1mu}\beta}^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k}
in (
3.1
), we write

S^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k}
for the determinantal
R({\mspace{1mu}\beta})
-module such that

\Upomega([S^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k}])=D(w^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k}\varpi_{i_{k}},w^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k^{-}}\varpi_{i_{k}}).

We sometimes write
S^{\textbf{{i}}}({\mspace{1mu}\beta})
for
S^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k}
to emphasize
{\mspace{1mu}\beta}
. Note that

{\rm wt}(S^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k})={\rm wt}(D(w^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k}\varpi_{i_{k}},w^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k^{-}}\varpi_{i_{k}}))=-{\mspace{1mu}\beta}.

The module
S^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k}
is also called the
cuspidal module
associated with
i
and
{\mspace{1mu}\beta}
. For another reduced sequence
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}
of
w_{\circ}
such that
\textbf{{i}}\overset{c}{\sim}\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}
, we have
S^{\textbf{{i}}}({\mspace{1mu}\beta})\simeq S^{\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}}({\mspace{1mu}\beta})
. Thus
the notation
S^{[\textbf{{i}}]}({\mspace{1mu}\beta})
make sense.

Proposition 6.5

(
[
29
,
35
]
)
.

Let
M
and
N
be simple modules such that one of them is affreal.
Then there exists a unique
R
-module homomorphism
\mathbf{r}_{M,N}\in\mathrm{H{\scriptstyle OM}}_{R}(M,N)
satisfying

\mathrm{H{\scriptstyle OM}}_{R}(M\mathop{\mathbin{\mbox{\large$\circ$}}}N,N\mathop{\mathbin{\mbox{\large$\circ$}}}M)=\mathbf{k}\,\mathbf{r}_{M,N}.

We call the homomorphism
\mathbf{r}_{M,N}
the
R
-matrix
.

Definition 6.6
.

For simple
R
-modules
M
and
N
such that one of them is affreal, we define

\displaystyle\Lambda(M,N)

\displaystyle\mathbin{:=}\deg(\mathbf{r}_{M,N}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Lambda}(M,N)

\displaystyle\mathbin{:=}\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\Lambda(M,N)+\bigl({\rm wt}(M),{\rm wt}(N)\bigr)\Bigr),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(M,N)

\displaystyle\mathbin{:=}\frac{1}{2}\big(\Lambda(M,N)+\Lambda(N,M)\big).

It is proved in
[
29
,
35
]
that the invariants
\widetilde{\Lambda}(M,N)
and
\mathfrak{d}(M,N)
in Definition
6.6
belong to
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}
.

For simple modules
M
and
N
, let
M\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}N
and
M\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\Delta$}}N
denote the head and the socle of
M\mathop{\mathbin{\mbox{\large$\circ$}}}N
, respectively.

Proposition 6.7

(
[
28
, Lemma 3.1.4]
,
[
29
, Lemma 4.1.2]
)
.

Let
M
and
N
be self-dual simple
R
-modules such that one of them is affreal.

(a)

M\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}N
and
M\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\Delta$}}N
are simple modules and they appear exactly once in the composition series of
M\mathop{\mathbin{\mbox{\large$\circ$}}}N
.

(b)

q^{\widetilde{\Lambda}(M,N)}M\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}N
is a self-dual simple module.

(c)

If
M\mathop{\mathbin{\mbox{\large$\circ$}}}N\simeq N\mathop{\mathbin{\mbox{\large$\circ$}}}M
up to a grading shift, then
M\mathop{\mathbin{\mbox{\large$\circ$}}}N
is simple, which is equivalent to
\mathfrak{d}(M,N)=0
and
M\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}N\simeq M\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\Delta$}}N
up to a grading shift.

For a reduced sequence
i
of
w_{\circ}
, we say an interval
[a,b]\subset[1,\ell]
is an
i
-box
if
i_{a}=i_{b}
.
For each

i
-box
[a,b]
and
i
, we define a self-dual simple modules
L^{\textbf{{i}}}[a,b]
as follows:
L^{\textbf{{i}}}[a,a]\mathbin{:=}S^{\textbf{{i}}}_{a}
and

L^{\textbf{{i}}}[a,b]\mathbin{:=}q^{\widetilde{\Lambda}\big(S^{\textbf{{i}}}_{b},L^{\textbf{{i}}}[a,b^{-}]\big)}S^{\textbf{{i}}}_{b}\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}L^{\textbf{{i}}}[a,b^{-}].

Proposition 6.8

(
[
32
, Proposition 4.6]
)
.

For a reduced sequence
\textbf{{i}}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell})
of
w_{\circ}
and an
i
-box
[a,b]\subset[1,\ell]
, we have

\Upomega\left(\big[L^{\textbf{{i}}}[a,b]\big]\right)=D(w_{b}^{\textbf{{i}}}\varpi_{i_{b}},w_{a^{-}}^{\textbf{{i}}}\varpi_{i_{a}}).

Hence
L^{\textbf{{i}}}[a,b]
is an affreal simple module.

6.3.
Laurent family

Let
J
be an index set and
\mathscr{C}
be a full monoidal subcategory of
R\text{-}\mathrm{gmod}
stable by taking subquotients, extensions and grading shifts.
We say that a family of affreal modules
\mathcal{L}=\{L_{j}\}
in
\mathscr{C}
is a commuting family in
\mathscr{C}
if

L_{i}\mathop{\mathbin{\mbox{\large$\circ$}}}L_{j}\simeq L_{j}\mathop{\mathbin{\mbox{\large$\circ$}}}L_{i}\quad\text{ up to a grading shift for any $i,j\in J$.}

Then there exists a family
\{\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a})\ |\ {\bm{a}}\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}_{0}^{\oplus J}\}
of simple modules in
\mathscr{C}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(0)=\mathbf{1},\quad\mathcal{L}({\bm{e}}_{j})=L_{j}\quad\text{ for any }j\in J,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}({\bm{a}})\mathop{\mathbin{\mbox{\large$\circ$}}}\mathcal{L}({\bm{b}})\simeq q^{-\widetilde{\Lambda}(\mathcal{L}({\bm{a}}),\mathcal{L}({\bm{b}}))}\mathcal{L}({\bm{a}}+{\bm{b}})\quad\text{ for any }{\bm{a}},{\bm{b}}\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}_{0}^{\oplus J}.

We say a commuting family
\mathcal{L}=\{L_{j}\}_{j\in J}
is
independent
if, when
{\bm{a}},{\bm{b}}\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}_{0}^{\oplus J}
satisfies
\mathcal{L}({\bm{a}})\simeq q^{s}\mathcal{L}({\bm{b}})

for some
s\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
, we have
{\bm{a}}={\bm{b}}
.

Definition 6.9
.

A commuting family
\mathcal{L}=\{L_{j}\mid j\in J\}

of affreal simple objects of
\mathscr{C}
is said to be a
quasi-Laurent family
in
\mathscr{C}
if

\mathcal{L}
satisfies the following conditions:

(a)

\mathcal{L}
is independent, and

(b)

if a simple module
X
commutes with all
L_{j}

(j\in J)
,
then there exist
{\bm{a}},{\bm{b}}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}^{J}
such that

X\mathop{\mathbin{\mbox{\large$\circ$}}}\mathcal{L}({\bm{a}})\simeq\mathcal{L}({\bm{b}})\quad\text{up to a grading shift}.

If
\mathcal{M}
satisfies (
a
) and (
c
) below, then we say that
\mathcal{L}

is a
Laurent family
:

(c)

if a simple module
X
commutes with all
L_{j}

(j\in J)
,
then there exists
{\bm{b}}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}^{J}
such that
X\simeq\mathcal{L}({\bm{b}})
up to a grading shift.

For an index set
J=J_{\mathrm{ex}}\sqcup J_{\mathrm{fr}}
,
assume that
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathscr{C})\mathbin{:=}\mathbb{A}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1}]}K(\mathscr{C})
is isomorphic to a quantum cluster algebra
\mathscr{A}
such that

\Upomega(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathscr{C}))\simeq\mathscr{A}
.
We say that a commuting family
\mathcal{L}=\{L_{i}\}_{i\in J}
in
\mathscr{C}
is a
monoidal cluster
in
\mathscr{C}
if there exists
a seed
(\{Z_{i}\}_{i\in J},\Lambda=(\Lambda_{i,j})_{i,j\in J},\widetilde{B}=(b_{i,j})_{i\in J,j\in J_{\mathrm{ex}}})
of
\mathscr{A}
such that

Z_{i}\cong\Upomega([L_{i}])\ \ \text{ in $\mathscr{A}$ }\quad\text{ and }\quad\Lambda_{i,j}=-\Lambda(L_{i},L_{j}).

Theorem 6.10

(
[
37
, Proposition 3.6]
)
.

Assume that
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathscr{C})
has a quantum cluster algebra structure, and let

\mathcal{L}=\{L_{j}\mid j\in J\}
be a monoidal cluster in
\mathscr{C}
. Then the commuting family
\mathcal{L}

is a quasi-Laurent family.
In particular,
the isomorphism class
[X]
of a module
X
in
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathscr{C})

can be expressed as an element in the Laurent polynomial
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1}][\;[L_{j}]\mid j\in J]
with positive coefficients. Moreover, if every
[L_{k}]
is prime in
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathscr{C})|_{q=1}
,
then
\mathcal{L}
is a Laurent family.

Remark 6.11
.

Note that if
K(\mathscr{C})|_{q=1}
is factorial, then every
[L_{k}]
is prime in
K(\mathscr{C})|_{q=1}
(see
[
14
]
).

For a reduced sequence
i
of
w_{\circ}
and
1\leqslant k\leqslant\ell
, we set

L^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k}\mathbin{:=}L(w^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k}\varpi_{i_{k}},\varpi_{i_{k}})\simeq L^{\textbf{{i}}}[k^{\min},k].

Proposition 6.12

(
[
32
, Theorem 4.12]
)
.

Let
i
be a reduced sequence of
w_{\circ}
. For
1\leqslant s<t\leqslant\ell
, we have

-\Lambda(L^{\textbf{{i}}}_{s},L^{\textbf{{i}}}_{t})=(\varpi_{i_{s}}-w^{\textbf{{i}}}_{s}\varpi_{i_{s}},\varpi_{i_{t}}+w^{\textbf{{i}}}_{t}\varpi_{i_{t}}).

Hence
\mathcal{L}^{\textbf{{i}}}\mathbin{:=}\{L^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k}\}
forms a commuting family.

7.
Quantum positivity

In this section, we give a partial proof of the quantum positivity conjecture (Conjecture
1
(
i
)) for non-simply-laced type. Thus throughout this section, we assume that
\mathfrak{g}
is of non-simply-laced type.

Recall the following:

(7.1)

(a)

For any reduced sequence
i
of
w_{\circ}
, there exists an
\mathbb{A}
-algebra isomorphism

\varphi_{\textbf{{i}}}:\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathfrak{n})

sending
Z_{k}
to
\widetilde{D}(w^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k}\varpi_{i_{k}},\varpi_{i_{k}})
. Moreover, this isomorphism
sends the seed
(\{Z_{k}\}_{1\leqslant k\leqslant\ell},\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}},\widetilde{B}^{\textbf{{i}}})
of
\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}}
to
the seed
(\{\widetilde{D}(w^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k}\varpi_{i_{k}},\varpi_{i_{k}})\}_{1\leqslant k\leqslant\ell},\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}},\widetilde{B}^{\textbf{{i}}})
of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathfrak{n})
.

(b)

There exists an
\mathbb{A}
-algebra monomorphism
(\cdot)_{\leqslant\xi}:\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}\to\mathcal{X}_{q,\leqslant\xi}

for each Dynkin quiver
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
. Thus the image
(\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q})_{\leqslant\xi}
is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}

as
\mathbb{A}
-algebras. Moreover
(F_{q}(m^{(i)}[p,\xi_{i}])_{\leqslant\xi}=\underline{m^{(i)}[p,\xi_{i}]}
for
(i,p)\in\Gamma^{Q}_{0}
.

(c)

For a reduced sequence
i
of
w_{\circ}
adapted to a Dynkin quiver
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
, there exists an
\mathbb{A}
-algebra isomorphism

\kappa_{\textbf{{i}}}:\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}

sending the seed
(\{Z_{k}\}_{1\leqslant k\leqslant\ell},\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}},\widetilde{B}^{\textbf{{i}}})
of
\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{{i}}}
to
the one
(\{F_{q}([p_{k},\xi_{i_{k}}]\}_{1\leqslant k\leqslant\ell},\Lambda^{\textbf{{i}}},\widetilde{B}^{\textbf{{i}}})
of
\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}
, where
{\widetilde{\textbf{{i}}}}(k)=(i_{k},p_{k})
.

(d)

For a reduced sequence
i
of
w_{\circ}
adapted to a Dynkin quiver
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
, Proposition
6.8
tells us that

\Upomega(L^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k})\cong\widetilde{D}(w^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k}\varpi_{i_{k}},\varpi_{i_{k}})\quad\text{ for all }1\leqslant k\leqslant\ell.

Thus, for a reduced sequence
i
of
w_{\circ}
adapted to a Dynkin quiver
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
, we have the following commutative diagram
of isomorphisms

\textstyle{\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{A}}(R\text{-}\mathrm{gmod})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Xi_{Q,\leqslant\xi}}
\scriptstyle{\Xi_{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\Upomega}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathfrak{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{Q}}
\textstyle{(\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q})_{\leqslant\xi}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\cdot)_{\leqslant\xi}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{ii}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{\textbf{{i}}}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{\textbf{{i}}}}

such that

\Xi_{Q}\mathbin{:=}\Psi_{Q}\circ\Upomega:\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{A}}(R\text{-}\mathrm{gmod})\mathop{\xrightarrow[\raisebox{0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.90417pt]{$\scriptstyle{}$}]{{\raisebox{-1.80835pt}[0.0pt][-1.80835pt]{$\mspace{2mu}\sim\mspace{2mu}$}}}}\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q}\quad\text{ and }\quad\Xi_{Q,\leqslant\xi}\mathbin{:=}(\cdot)_{\leqslant\xi}\circ\Xi_{Q}:\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{A}}(R\text{-}\mathrm{gmod})\mathop{\xrightarrow[\raisebox{0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.90417pt]{$\scriptstyle{}$}]{{\raisebox{-1.80835pt}[0.0pt][-1.80835pt]{$\mspace{2mu}\sim\mspace{2mu}$}}}}(\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q})_{\leqslant\xi}.

Moreover, the isomorphism
\Xi_{Q,\leqslant\xi}
sends

L(w^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k}\varpi_{k},\varpi_{k})
for
1\leqslant k\leqslant\ell
to
q^{a/2}\underline{m^{(i)}[p,\xi_{i}]}
for some
a\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}
, where
{\widetilde{\textbf{{i}}}}(k)=(i,p)\in\Gamma^{Q}_{0}
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
be a Dynkin quiver.
For an element
\mathsf{X}_{\leqslant\xi}\in(\mathfrak{K}_{q,Q})_{\leqslant\xi}
, assume that there exists a module
X\in R\text{-}\mathrm{gmod}
and
\Xi_{Q,\leqslant\xi}([X])\cong\mathsf{X}_{\leqslant\xi}
. Write

\mathsf{X}_{\leqslant\xi}=\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}^{Q}}a[m;m^{\prime}]\underline{m}^{\prime}\quad\text{ for }a[m:m^{\prime}]\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}[q^{\pm 1/2}].

Then
\mathsf{X}_{\leqslant\xi}
is quantum positive; that is

a[m;m^{\prime}]\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}_{0}[q^{\pm 1/2}].

Proof.

By Proposition
6.12
and (
7.1
),

\text{ the commuting family }\mathcal{L}^{\textbf{{i}}}\text{ is a monoidal cluster of $R\text{-}\mathrm{gmod}$}.

Hence Theorem
6.10
tells us that

[X]=\sum_{{\bm{a}}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\ell}}\alpha(X;{\bm{a}})\prod^{\to}_{r\in[1,\ell]}[L_{r}^{\textbf{{i}}}]^{{\bm{a}}_{r}}\quad\text{for some $\alpha(X;{\bm{a}})\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}_{0}[q^{\pm 1}]$.}

Now let us take a reduced expression
i
of
w_{\circ}
adapted to the Dynkin quiver
Q
. Then, for
1\leqslant k\leqslant\ell
with
{\widetilde{\textbf{{i}}}}(k)=(i_{k},p_{k})
, we have

\Xi_{Q,\leqslant\xi}(L^{\textbf{{i}}}_{k})\cong\underline{m^{(i)}[p_{k},\xi_{i_{k}}]}\in\mathcal{M}^{+}_{Q}
. Hence

\mathsf{X}_{\leqslant\xi}\cong\Xi_{Q,\leqslant\xi}([X])=\sum_{{\bm{a}}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\ell}}\alpha^{\prime}(X;{\bm{a}})\prod^{\to}_{r\in[1,\ell]}\underline{m^{(i)}[p_{r},\xi_{i_{r}}]}^{{\bm{a}}_{r}}\quad\text{ where $\alpha^{\prime}(X;{\bm{a}})\cong\alpha^{\prime}(X;{\bm{a}})$.}

Since
\displaystyle\prod^{\to}_{r\in[1,\ell]}\underline{m^{(i)}[p_{r},\xi_{i_{r}}]}^{{\bm{a}}_{r}}\in\mathcal{M}_{Q}
for any
{\bm{a}}\in\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}^{\ell}
, our assertion follows.
∎

The following corollary provides a partial proof of Conjecture
1
(
i
).

Corollary 7.2
.

Let
m
be a KR-monomial contained in
\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{+}
for some Dynkin quiver
Q=(\triangle,\xi)
. Then

(F_{q}(m))_{\leqslant\xi}
is quantum positive. In particular,
F_{q}(X_{i,p})
is quantum positive for every
(i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
, and hence, so are the elements in the standard basis
\mathsf{E}_{q}
.

Proof.

The first assertion is a direct consequence of Theorem
5.19
(
b
), Proposition
6.2
, (
7.1
)
and Theorem
7.1
.
Let us focus on the second assertion which gives
a complete proof of Conjecture
1
(
i
) for
m=X_{i,p}
. Recall that

i^{*}=i
for all
i\in I
because
\mathfrak{g}
is of non-simply-laced type. Hence

\Gamma^{Q}_{0}=\{(i,p)\in{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}\ |\ \xi_{i}-\mathsf{h}<p\leqslant\xi_{i}\}.

By the first assertion,

(F_{q}(X_{i,\xi_{i}-\mathsf{h}+2}))_{\leqslant\xi}
is quantum positive as an element in
\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}_{0}[q^{\pm 1/2}][\widetilde{X}_{i,p}^{\pm 1}]_{(i,p)\in\Gamma^{Q}_{0}}
.
On the other hand, Theorem
5.6
(
c
) implies that

(F_{q}(X_{i,\xi_{i}-\mathsf{h}+2}))_{\leqslant\xi}=F_{q}(X_{i,\xi_{i}-\mathsf{h}+2})-\underline{X_{i,\xi_{i}+2}^{-1}}.

Hence our assertion follows from the automorphism
\mathsf{T}_{r}
in Remark
5.5
.
∎

Example 7.3
.

(a)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be of type
B_{4}
. Then we have

\displaystyle F^{B_{4}}_{q}(X_{1,0})

\displaystyle=q\widetilde{X}_{1,0}+q\widetilde{X}_{2,1}*\widetilde{X}_{1,2}^{-1}+q\widetilde{X}_{3,2}*\widetilde{X}_{2,3}^{-1}+q^{2}\widetilde{X}_{4,3}^{2}*\widetilde{X}_{3,4}^{-1}+(q^{-1}+q)\widetilde{X}_{4,3}*\widetilde{X}_{4,5}^{-1}

\displaystyle\hskip 107.63855pt+q^{2}\widetilde{X}_{3,4}*\widetilde{X}_{4,5}^{-2}+q\widetilde{X}_{2,5}*\widetilde{X}_{3,6}^{-1}+q\widetilde{X}_{1,6}*\widetilde{X}_{2,7}^{-1}+q^{-1}\widetilde{X}_{1,8}^{-1},

where
q\widetilde{X}_{1,0}=\underline{X_{1,0}}
and
q^{-1}\widetilde{X}_{1,8}^{-1}=\underline{X_{1,8}^{-1}}
.

(b)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be of type
B_{3}
. Take
Q=(\triangle_{B_{3}},\xi)
with
\xi_{1}=\xi_{3}=0
and
\xi_{2}=-1
, and a simple module
X=L(2)\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}L(1)
in
R^{B_{3}}\text{-}\mathrm{gmod}
, where

L(1)
and
L(2)
are
1
-dimensional cuspidal module with their weights
{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{1}
and
{\mspace{1mu}\alpha}_{2}
, respectively. Note that the image
\mathsf{X}
of
X
under
\Xi_{Q}
is neither a fundamental polynomial nor a KR-polynomial,
while
\Xi_{Q}(L(1)\mathbin{\scalebox{.9}{$\nabla$}}L(2))\simeq F^{B_{3}}_{q}(X_{1,-4})
.
Then one can compute that

\displaystyle\Xi_{Q,\leqslant\xi}([X])=\mathsf{X}_{\leqslant\xi}=q\widetilde{X}_{2,-5}*\widetilde{X}_{1,0}+q^{4}\widetilde{X}_{1,-4}*\widetilde{X}_{3,-4}^{2}*\widetilde{X}_{2,-3}^{-1}*\widetilde{X}_{1,0}+q^{4}\widetilde{X}_{3,-4}^{2}*\widetilde{X}_{1,-2}^{-1}*\widetilde{X}_{1,0}

\displaystyle\qquad+(q+q^{3})\widetilde{X}_{1,-4}*\widetilde{X}_{3,-4}*\widetilde{X}_{3,-2}^{-1}*\widetilde{X}_{1,0}+q^{4}\widetilde{X}_{1,-4}*\widetilde{X}_{2,-3}*\widetilde{X}_{3,-2}^{-2}*\widetilde{X}_{1,0}

\displaystyle\qquad+(q^{3}+q^{5})\widetilde{X}_{3,-4}*\widetilde{X}_{2,-3}*\widetilde{X}_{1,-2}^{-1}*\widetilde{X}_{3,-2}^{-1}*\widetilde{X}_{1,0}+q^{8}\widetilde{X}_{2,-3}^{2}*\widetilde{X}_{1,-2}^{-1}*\widetilde{X}_{3,-2}^{-2}*\widetilde{X}_{1,0}

\displaystyle\qquad+q^{3}\widetilde{X}_{1,-4}*\widetilde{X}_{1,-2}*\widetilde{X}_{2,-1}^{-1}*\widetilde{X}_{1,0}+(q^{2}+q^{4})\widetilde{X}_{3,-4}*\widetilde{X}_{3,-2}*\widetilde{X}_{2,-1}^{-1}*\widetilde{X}_{1,0}

\displaystyle\qquad+(q+q^{5})\widetilde{X}_{2,-3}*\widetilde{X}_{2,-1}^{-1}*\widetilde{X}_{1,0}+q^{8}\widetilde{X}_{1,-2}*\widetilde{X}_{3,-2}^{2}*\widetilde{X}_{2,-1}^{-2}*\widetilde{X}_{1,0}+(q+q^{3})\widetilde{X}_{3,-4}*\widetilde{X}_{1,0}*\widetilde{X}_{3,0}^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad+(q^{2}+q^{4})\widetilde{X}_{2,-3}*\widetilde{X}_{3,-2}^{-1}*\widetilde{X}_{1,0}*\widetilde{X}_{3,0}^{-1}+(q^{3}+q^{5})\widetilde{X}_{1,-2}*\widetilde{X}_{3,-2}*\widetilde{X}_{2,-1}^{-1}*\widetilde{X}_{1,0}*\widetilde{X}_{3,0}^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad+q^{4}\widetilde{X}_{1,-2}*\widetilde{X}_{1,0}*\widetilde{X}_{3,0}^{-2},

all of whose coefficients are contained in
\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu}_{\geqslant 0}[q^{\pm 1/2}]
and
q\widetilde{X}_{2,-5}*\widetilde{X}_{1,0}=\underline{X_{2,-5}X_{1,1}}
.

Appendix A
Examples of substitution formula

In this appendix, we provide several examples of substitution formulas for
B_{2}
.
We consider the situation when
q=1
for simplicity. Its quantum analogue
\widetilde{\Psi}_{\mathfrak{Q},\mathfrak{Q}^{\prime}}
can be calculated in a similar manner.

Consider the height functions given by

\displaystyle\xi^{\prime}

\displaystyle:\{1,2\}\to\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu},\quad 1\mapsto 0,\quad 2\mapsto-1,

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle:\{1,2\}\to\mathbb{Z}\mspace{1mu},\quad 1\mapsto 0,\quad 2\mapsto 1.

Then the following infinite sequences
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}=(i^{\prime}_{u})_{u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}},\textbf{{i}}=(i_{u})_{u\in\mathbb{N}\mspace{1mu}}
are of the form in (
5.11
):

\displaystyle\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}=(\hskip-8.61108pt\underset{\text{\scalebox{0.8}{reduced word for $w_{\circ}$}}}{\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1,2,1,2,}}}\hskip-8.61108pt\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1,2,1,2}},\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1,2,1,2}},\dots),

\displaystyle\textbf{{i}}=(\hskip-8.61108pt\underset{\text{\scalebox{0.8}{reduced word for $w_{\circ}$}}}{\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2,1,2,1,}}}\hskip-8.61108pt\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2,1,2,1}},\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2,1,2,1,}},\dots).

Then

is the following quiver:

Here the correspondence of labelling of vertices is given by

(A.2)

\displaystyle(1,-4m)

\displaystyle\mapsto(1,-4m),\hskip 37.88878pt(1,-2-4m)\mapsto(1,-2-4m),

\displaystyle(2,1-4m)

\displaystyle\mapsto(2,-1-4m),\qquad(2,-1-4m)\mapsto(2,-3-4m).

Let us write the initial cluster variable for
i
as
\{Z_{i,p}\mathbin{:=}Z^{\xi}_{i,p}\}
and the one for
\textbf{{i}}^{\prime}
as
\{Z^{\prime}_{i,p}\mathbin{:=}Z^{\xi^{\prime}}_{i,p}\}
. Similarly, we write
the generators of

as
\{X_{i,p}\}

and the generators of
\mathcal{X}_{q,\leqslant\xi^{\prime}}
as
\{X^{\prime}_{i,p}\}
.

Using Keller’s mutation applet, the isomorphism
\widehat{{\bm{\tau}}}^{*}(Z^{\prime}_{(i,p)})
is computed as follows:

\displaystyle Z^{\prime}_{1,-4m}

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(Z_{2,-4m+3}^{2}Z_{1,-4m}^{2}+2Z_{2,-4m+3}Z_{1,-4m+2}Z_{1,-4m}Z_{2,-4m-1}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 21.52771pt\left.+Z_{1,-4m+2}^{2}Z_{2,-4m-1}^{2}+Z_{1,-4m+2}Z_{2,-4m+1}^{2}Z_{1,-4m-2}\right)/(Z_{2,-4m+1}^{2}Z_{1,-4m}),

\displaystyle Z^{\prime}_{2,-4m-1}

\displaystyle\mapsto\dfrac{Z_{2,-4m+3}Z_{1,-4m}Z_{2,-4m-1}+Z_{1,-4m+2}Z_{2,-4m-1}^{2}+Z_{2,-4m+1}^{2}Z_{1,-4m-2}}{Z_{2,-4m+1}Z_{1,-4m}},

\displaystyle Z^{\prime}_{1,-4m-2}

\displaystyle\mapsto Z_{1,-4m-2},\qquad Z^{\prime}_{2,-4m-3}\mapsto Z_{2,-4m-1}.

Hence

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}_{\xi^{\prime},\xi}(m^{(i)}[p,\xi^{\prime}_{i}])

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}m^{(1)}[-4m-2,0]&\text{ if }(i,p)=(1,-4m-2),\\
m^{(2)}[-4m-1,1]&\text{ if }(i,p)=(2,-4m-3),\\
\left(X_{2,-4m+1}^{-2}X_{1,-4m}+2X_{2,-4m-1}X_{2,-4m+1}^{-1}\right.\\
\hskip 51.6665pt\left.+X_{1,-4m}^{-1}X_{2,-4m-1}^{2}+X_{1,-4m-2}\right)m^{(1)}[-4m+2,0]&\text{ if }(i,p)=(1,-4m),\\
(X_{2,-4m+1}^{-1}+X_{1,-4m}^{-1}X_{2,-4m-1}+X_{1,-4m-2}X_{2,-4m-1}^{-1})m^{(2)}[-4m-1,1]&\text{ if }(i,p)=(2,-4m-1).\end{cases}

This calculation implies that, for
(i,p)\in{}^{\xi^{\prime}[-2]}{\widehat{\triangle}}_{0}
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}_{\xi^{\prime},\xi}(X^{\prime}_{i,p})=\widetilde{\Psi}_{\xi^{\prime},\xi}(m^{(i)}[p,\xi^{\prime}_{i}])\widetilde{\Psi}_{\xi^{\prime},\xi}(m^{(i)}[p+2,\xi^{\prime}_{i}])^{-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\left(X_{1,-4m-2}^{-1}X_{2,-4m+1}^{-2}+2X_{2,-4m-1}X_{2,-4m+1}^{-1}X_{1,-4m}^{-1}X_{1,-4m-2}^{-1}\right.\\
\hskip 86.11084pt\left.+X_{1,-4m}^{-2}X_{1,-4m-2}^{-1}X_{2,-4m-1}^{2}+X_{1,-4m}^{-1}\right)^{-1}&\text{ if }(i,p)=(1,-4m-2),\\
\left(X_{1,-4m}X_{2,-4m+1}^{-2}+2X_{2,-4m-1}X_{2,-4m+1}^{-1}+X_{1,-4m}^{-1}X_{2,-4m-1}^{2}+X_{1,-4m-2}\right)&\text{ if }(i,p)=(1,-4m),\\
(X_{2,-4m+1}^{-1}+X_{1,-4m}^{-1}X_{2,-4m-1}+X_{1,-4m-2}X_{2,-4m-1}^{-1})^{-1}&\text{ if }(i,p)=(2,-4m-3),\\
(X_{2,-4m-1}+X_{1,-4m}^{-1}X_{2,-4m-1}^{2}X_{2,-4m+1}+X_{1,-4m-2}X_{2,-4m+1})&\text{ if }(i,p)=(2,-4m-1),\end{cases}

which shows the substitution formula representing
T_{2}
on
\mathfrak{K}(B_{2})
. Then we can observe the following:
